```js
 * Code that no artifact builds, as `{ crate, path, reason }`: the notices leave out its license files (input 3) and the
 * crate archives of 2.13 leave out its files. `path` is relative to the crate's folder: a file, or a folder ending in
 * `/` for every file below it.
 */
export const EXCLUDED_LICENSE_FILES = [
  {
    crate: 'libdbus-sys',
    path: 'vendor/dbus/',
    reason: 'the vendored libdbus, compiled only with the vendored feature, which no Partsmith build enables',
  },
]

/** The crates allowed to ship prebuilt libraries (input 8), as `{ crate, reason }`. */
export const PREBUILT_LIBRARY_CRATES = [
  {
    crate: 'webview2-com-sys',
    reason: 'links the WebView2 SDK loader statically under MSVC; the loader has its own entry (input 6)',
  },
  {
    crate: 'windows_x86_64_msvc',
    reason: 'import libraries that the windows-rs project generates, under the crate license',
  },
]

/**
 * The versions of the code the NSIS installer adds to the Windows setup executable (input 6): the tauri-cli of
 * section 2.2, the tauri-bundler its lock file pins, and the NSIS and nsis_tauri_utils that tauri-bundler downloads.
 */
export const WINDOWS_INSTALLER = {
  tauriCli: '2.12.2',
  tauriBundler: '2.10.2',
  nsis: '3.11',
  nsisTauriUtils: '0.5.3',
}

/** `text` normalized as below; '' when nothing is left. */
export function normalizeText(text)

/**
 * The external crates of one artifact (input 1): for each of `triples`, in order, the parsed output of the command of
 * input 1 for that triple, sent through `run` in `root`, and the crates `rootName` builds in it. Each crate is once in
 * the result, as `artifactCrates` gives it, sorted as `artifactCrates` sorts. The dependency source archives take
 * their crate set from it (2.13).
 */
export async function scopeCrates(root, run, triples, rootName)

/**
 * `paragraph`, one line, with its words filled into lines of at most 80 characters (Wrapping below), joined with LF. A
 * no-break space joins two words into one, and the lines hold a space in its place.
 */
export function wrapParagraph(paragraph)

/**
 * The external crates that `rootName` builds in one parsed `cargo metadata` output (input 1), each once as
 * `{ name, version, license, licenseFile, folder, repository }`, sorted by name and then by version precedence.
 */
export function artifactCrates(metadata, rootName)

/**
 * The paths of the license files of a package folder (input 3), relative to it with `/`, in order, without the
 * paths `excluded` names (written as the `path` of EXCLUDED_LICENSE_FILES).
 */
export async function licenseFiles(folder, licenseFile, excluded = [])

/** The prebuilt libraries of a package folder (input 8), relative to it with `/`, in byte order. */
export async function prebuiltLibraries(folder)

/** The text of one notices file from its parts, in the layout below, with no input or output. */
export function renderNotices(notices)

/**
 * The text of the notices file of a native npm package (2.13): the entry line of each of `entries`, entries as
 * `renderNotices` takes them, and then each distinct text once, numbered in the order the lines first name it.
 */
export function renderCrateNotices(entries)

/** Both texts as `{ desktop, web }`, read from the repository at `root`, with every command sent through `run`. */
export async function generateNotices(root, { run })
```

`renderNotices` takes `{ heading, scope, partsmith, occt, fonts, crates, packages, libraries }`. `partsmith` is the text of `LICENSE`; `occt` is `{ statement, entries }`; `occt.entries` and the other four are lists of entries `{ name, version, license, url, platforms, textSource, texts }`, where `texts` holds the entry's normalized license texts in order, `platforms` the desktop platforms whose graph holds it (`null` in the web file), and `textSource` where its texts come from: `shipped` (from the package itself or a file inputs 4 to 7 name), `standard` or `repository` (input 3).

**Inputs.** Every input is fixed by committed files:

1. **Crates.** For each triple, `cargo metadata --format-version 1 --locked --all-features --filter-platform <triple>` in the repository root, which is the graph `cargo-deny` checks (`deny.toml` sets `[graph] all-features`). An artifact's crates are the packages with a non-null `source` that `resolve` reaches from its root crate through dependencies whose `dep_kinds` include a normal one (`kind` null) or a `build` one. Dev-dependencies are left out, and the workspace crates are Partsmith itself. Release bundles build on their own operating system, so the target filter is right for build dependencies too. The desktop crates are the union over the three triples, each with the platforms whose graph holds it. The root crate's `repository` names the Partsmith repository in the statements.
2. **npm packages.** `shippedPackages` of 2.10: the production packages that `pnpm licenses list --prod --json` lists in `ui/`, and the build packages of `EMITTED_BUILD_PACKAGES` whose code the build writes into `ui/dist`, each name and version once. Both files list them, since the desktop app embeds the same `ui/dist`.
3. **License texts of a crate or package**, in this order, each group in byte order of paths and each file once: the file its manifest names as `license_file` (crates only); every regular file directly in its folder (the folder of `manifest_path`, or the npm package's path) whose name starts with `LICENSE`, `LICENCE`, `COPYING`, `COPYRIGHT`, `NOTICE`, `UNLICENSE`, `THIRD-PARTY`, `THIRD_PARTY` or `THIRDPARTY` in any letter case (the last three for the notices of code a package bundles from other projects, such as the rollup and esbuild texts of `rolldown`'s `THIRD-PARTY-LICENSE`); every regular file directly in a folder `LICENSES` there; and every other regular file below the folder, at any depth, whose name starts with one of those prefixes and does not end in `.rs`, `.c`, `.h`, `.map` or a JavaScript or TypeScript extension (`.js`, `.mjs`, `.cjs`, `.jsx`, `.ts`, `.mts`, `.cts`, `.tsx`), unless `EXCLUDED_LICENSE_FILES` names it for that crate. The dependency source archives leave out the same paths (2.13, Files left out). An entry therefore names only code that no Partsmith build compiles. The files with those endings are source code, such as the icon `dist/esm/icons/copyright.mjs` of `lucide-react`. Symbolic links are not followed. Nested files hold the licenses of code or data inside a crate, such as the Unicode data license of `regex-syntax` in `src/unicode_tables/LICENSE-UNICODE`. A package with none of these files gets one of two kinds of text, and its entry says which:
   - **Standard text**, when `Apache-2.0` or `MPL-2.0` alone satisfies its expression (the whole expression, or an `OR` alternative at any position), `Apache-2.0` first: that license's text from `ui/scripts/notices/licenses/<identifier>.txt`, a committed copy of the SPDX License List's plain text. These two texts have no copyright line to fill in.
   - **Text from its repository** otherwise: every regular file in `ui/scripts/notices/crates/<name>/`, in byte order of names, a committed copy of the license file in the crate's repository, kept by name so that a version update keeps it. A copy of a Markdown or JSON file gets `.txt` appended (the `LICENSE.md` of the objc2 repository is `LICENSE.md.txt`), because oxfmt formats those files under `ui/` and would change the text. The SPDX texts of MIT, BSD and Zlib leave the copyright line as a placeholder, and those licenses ask for "the above copyright notice", so only the holder's own file meets them.

   On 2026-10-16, 22 desktop crates ship no license file (ADR 0033). Twelve take a standard text: `Apache-2.0` for `defmt-parser`, `dispatch2`, `libappindicator-sys`, `objc2-app-kit`, `objc2-core-foundation`, `objc2-core-graphics`, `objc2-exception-helper`, `objc2-io-surface`, `objc2-quartz-core`, `objc2-web-kit` and `tauri-plugin`, and `MPL-2.0` for `selectors`. Ten take a text from their repository: `alloc-stdlib` (BSD-3-Clause), and `block2`, `dlopen2`, `dlopen2_derive`, `objc2`, `objc2-encode`, `objc2-foundation`, `webview2-com`, `webview2-com-macros` and `webview2-com-sys` (MIT). Every web crate ships its texts.
4. **OCCT.** `OCCT_VERSION` and `OCCT_TAG` from `scripts/occt-version.sh`, and the folder `ui/scripts/notices/occt-{OCCT_VERSION}/`, made once per OCCT pin and committed:
   - `LICENSE_LGPL_21.txt` and `OCCT_LGPL_EXCEPTION.txt`, copies of the files of those names in `share/doc/opencascade` of an install that `scripts/build-occt.sh` makes (the root of the tag's source holds the same two files).
   - `bundled.json`, an array of `{ name, license, path, text }`, one element for each piece of code from another project that the toolkits `partsmith-occt-sys/build.rs` links compile and whose license asks for its notice with binaries. `license` is an SPDX expression or `null`, `path` is the file in OCCT's source, and `text` names the file of this folder that holds its notice: the lines below, with each line's comment marker (`//`, `/*` or `*`) and the spaces after it removed.

   For 7.9.3, in this order:

   | `name` | `license` | `path` | `text`, from the lines |
   | --- | --- | --- | --- |
   | `flex, in OCCT's STEP reader (FlexLexer.h and the generated scanner)` | `null` | `src/FlexLexer/FlexLexer.h` | `FlexLexer.txt`, lines 5 to 28 |
   | `DELABELLA, in OCCT's mesher and in its copy in partsmith-occt-sys` | `MIT` | `src/BRepMesh/delabella.cpp` | `delabella.txt`, lines 3 to 24 |
   | `strtod of David M. Gay, in OCCT's number parsing` | `null` | `src/Standard/Standard_Strtod.cxx` | `strtod.txt`, lines 3 to 16 |
   | `ConvertUTF of Unicode, Inc., in OCCT's UTF iterator` | `null` | `src/NCollection/NCollection_UtfIterator.lxx` | `ConvertUTF.txt`, lines 16 to 36 |

   `partsmith-occt-sys` triangulates with a copy of that `delabella.cpp` (kernel.md 3.7 item 2, ADR 0039), which keeps the file's notice word for word, so the one entry and its text cover both.

   Not listed: the Bison skeleton of the STEP parser (`src/StepFile/step.tab.cxx`), whose special exception asks nothing, and the DejaVu Sans font of `src/Font`, compiled only with FreeType, which `build-occt.sh` turns off. Whoever adds the folder for a new pin also searches that version's sources of the linked toolkits for other third-party notices, and so does a change to the toolkits or the `-DUSE_*` options of `scripts/build-occt.sh` or to the toolkits that `crates/partsmith-occt-sys/build.rs` links, since either changes what OCCT compiles in (`USE_FREETYPE=ON` would add DejaVu Sans). A missing folder or file fails the generator.
5. **Fonts** (ADR 0031), named in the generator in this order: `Geist` `1.800`, `OFL-1.1`, `https://github.com/vercel/geist-font`, text `ui/public/fonts/Geist-OFL.txt`; `JetBrains Mono` `2.304`, `OFL-1.1`, `https://github.com/JetBrains/JetBrainsMono`, text `ui/public/fonts/JetBrainsMono-OFL.txt`.
6. **Other libraries**, in this order:
   - In both files: `Rust standard library` with the `channel` of `rust-toolchain.toml` as its version, `(MIT OR Apache-2.0) AND Unicode-3.0`, `https://www.rust-lang.org`, with two texts: the Apache License 2.0 (the text of `LICENSE`), and `ui/scripts/notices/rust-{channel}/Unicode-3.0.txt`, a copy of the toolchain's `share/doc/rust/licenses/Unicode-3.0.txt` (under `rustc --print sysroot`), for the Unicode data tables of `core` that the toolchain's `share/doc/rust/COPYRIGHT-library.html` names.
   - In the desktop file only, for Windows only: `Microsoft Edge WebView2 SDK loader, in webview2-com-sys` with the locked version of that crate, `BSD-3-Clause`, `https://www.nuget.org/packages/Microsoft.Web.WebView2`, text `ui/scripts/notices/webview2-com-sys-{version}/LICENSE.txt`, a copy of the `LICENSE.txt` of the `Microsoft.Web.WebView2` NuGet package whose `WebView2LoaderStatic.lib` that crate version ships and links statically under MSVC. The crate declares only MIT, which covers its bindings, and ships no text for the loader.
   - In the desktop file only, for Windows only, right after the WebView2 loader: `Microsoft Visual C++ runtime (libcmt, libvcruntime and libcpmt), in the Windows app` with no version, `see texts`, `https://visualstudio.microsoft.com/license-terms/`, text `ui/scripts/notices/msvc-runtime/Distributable-Code.txt` (the owner's decision of 2026-11-05, section 8). The linker copies this code into `partsmith-desktop.exe` from the static libraries of the Visual Studio that builds the release: the Visual C++ runtime (`libcmt.lib` and `libvcruntime.lib`) and the C++ standard library (`libcpmt.lib`), which OCCT, the shim and every Rust crate link on Windows (3.6). Microsoft ships them under the Distributable Code terms of the Visual Studio license terms, with no notice text of their own. The text is Partsmith's own. The entry's text source is `shipped` (input 6 names the file). It follows CPython's `PC/crtlicense.txt`, which its Windows build ships for the same code. It was written without Microsoft's page: neither `visualstudio.microsoft.com` nor `learn.microsoft.com` could be reached from the container that wrote it. A person checks it against Microsoft's terms before the first tag (open issue 10, test 127). The entry names no version. The version is the MSVC toolset of the `windows-2025` image, which changes with the image (MSVC 14.51.36231 of Visual Studio 2026 Enterprise in platforms run 14 on 2026-11-04). The entry and its text do not name the Universal C Runtime, which the app takes from Windows and does not carry (3.6 item 4). The file holds the three paragraphs below in this order, each as the lines `wrapParagraph` gives for it, with one blank line between two paragraphs and one LF at the end (test 125):

     ```text
     The Windows app of Partsmith, partsmith-desktop.exe, contains code from the Microsoft Visual C++ runtime (libcmt and libvcruntime) and the Microsoft C++ standard library (libcpmt), copyright Microsoft Corporation. The linker copied this code into the app from the static libraries of the Visual Studio that built it. It is Distributable Code under the Microsoft Visual Studio license terms. It is not open source software, and no other license in this file covers it.

     If you distribute the Windows app, or another program that contains this code, follow the Distributable Code terms of that license. Among other things, they ask you to distribute the code only in a program that adds significant primary functionality to it, to have the people you distribute it to agree to terms that protect it and Microsoft at least as much as Microsoft's terms do, not to use Microsoft's trademarks in a way that suggests your program comes from Microsoft or is endorsed by it, and not to put any part of the code under a license that requires it to be shared as source code or lets others modify it.

     Microsoft's license terms decide, not this summary. They cover only Microsoft's code: Partsmith, OCCT and every other part of the app keep the licenses listed in this file. The terms are at https://visualstudio.microsoft.com/license-terms/, one set for each edition and version of Visual Studio.
     ```

   - In the desktop file only, for Windows only, the code the NSIS installer adds to the setup executable (ADR 0034 question 3, answered with the strictest reading on 2026-11-04), in this order, with the versions of `WINDOWS_INSTALLER` (`{nsis}`, `{nsisTauriUtils}`, `{tauriBundler}`):
     - `NSIS with its zlib decompressor, Modern UI 2 and the plugins System, nsDialogs, StartMenu and NSISdl, in the Windows installer` `{nsis}`, `Zlib`, `https://sourceforge.net/projects/nsis/files/NSIS%203/{nsis}/`, texts `ui/scripts/notices/nsis-{nsis}/COPYING`, `ModernUI2-License.txt` and `NSISdl-License.txt`. They are copies of `COPYING`, `Docs/Modern UI 2/License.txt` and `Docs/NSISdl/License.txt` of `nsis-{nsis}.zip`. The copy of `COPYING` holds its lines 1 to 14 and 19 to 30: the copyright line of NSIS, the lines that put NSIS's code, plugins and graphics and its zlib compression module under the zlib/libpng license, and that license's text. The installer holds neither NSIS's bzip2 module nor its LZMA module (desktop.md 2.8). The copy leaves out lines 15 to 18, which name the two modules and their licenses, and lines 31 to 144, which hold the bzip2 license, the Common Public License 1.0 and the special exception for the LZMA module (test 126). The other two hold the copyright lines of Modern UI 2 and of NSISdl. The release writes the three files with CRLF line endings, and the © of the Modern UI 2 text as the Windows-1252 byte 0xA9, which normalized text would turn into U+FFFD. The copies hold LF line endings and that © in UTF-8, and are otherwise as shipped, `COPYING` with only those lines. The URL is the release's folder, which also holds its source, `nsis-{nsis}-src.tar.bz2` (open issue 10).
     - `nsis_tauri_utils, Tauri's NSIS plugin, with the crates semver and windows-sys, in the Windows installer` `{nsisTauriUtils}`, `MIT OR Apache-2.0`, `https://github.com/tauri-apps/nsis-tauri-utils/tree/nsis_tauri_utils-v{nsisTauriUtils}`, text from its repository: `ui/scripts/notices/nsis_tauri_utils-{nsisTauriUtils}/LICENSE_MIT` and `LICENSE_APACHE-2.0`, in this order, the order of its license expression, copies of the files of those names at that tag. The plugin is a `no_std` Rust library. Its DLL holds code of `semver` 1 and `windows-sys` 0.61, both under `MIT OR Apache-2.0`. Their exact versions in the DLL are not known: the repository commits no `Cargo.lock`. The desktop file lists both crates among its Rust crates with their texts, which this entry does not repeat.
     - `NSIS installer script of tauri-bundler, in the Windows installer` `{tauriBundler}`, `Apache-2.0 OR MIT`, `https://crates.io/crates/tauri-bundler/{tauriBundler}`, texts `ui/scripts/notices/tauri-bundler-{tauriBundler}/License-Apache.md.txt` and `License-MIT.md.txt`, copies of the crate's own `License-Apache.md` and `License-MIT.md` with `.txt` appended (input 3). They hold the copyright lines of the Cargo-Bundle developers and of the Tauri Apps contributors. The crate is not in `Cargo.lock`. The generator reads the copies. The entry's text source is `shipped`. It is `installer.nsi`, `utils.nsh` and the English strings of the crate's `src/bundle/windows/nsis/`, which NSIS compiles into the setup executable and its uninstaller. Its `FileAssociation.nsh` is not listed: it adds code only for file associations, and `tauri.conf.json` sets none.

     Where these come from: `tauri-cli` 2.12.2 (2.2) builds with tauri-bundler 2.10.2, the version its `Cargo.lock` pins. That version's `src/bundle/windows/nsis/mod.rs` downloads `nsis-3.11.zip` (SHA-1 `ef7ff767e5cbd9edd22add3a32c9b8f4500bb10d`, the same bytes as the upstream release on SourceForge) and `nsis_tauri_utils.dll` 0.5.3 (SHA-1 `75197fee3c6a814fe035788d1c34ead39349b860`). Its `installer.nsi` calls the plugins System, nsDialogs, StartMenu (the Modern UI page `MUI_PAGE_STARTMENU`), NSISdl (the download of the WebView2 bootstrapper, Tauri's default `webviewInstallMode`) and `nsis_tauri_utils` (`SemverCompare`, `RunAsUser` and `StrReplace`). It compresses with zlib: `tauri.conf.json` sets `bundle.windows.nsis.compression` to `zlib` in place of Tauri's default, LZMA (desktop.md 2.8). makensis then builds the setup executable and its uninstaller on the stub `Stubs/zlib_solid-x86-unicode` of `nsis-3.11.zip`. Neither holds the LZMA module, which is only in the zip's `lzma` stubs. The plugins are data of the setup executable, which the stub decompresses, and hold no compression code. A change of that setting changes the NSIS entry and its texts in the same pull request (test 126). A change of the `tauri-cli` version of 2.2 reads the new tauri-bundler's `src/bundle/windows/nsis/` again and updates `WINDOWS_INSTALLER` and the copies. Test 96 fails until `tauriCli` names that version.

     Nothing else the bundle formats add is third-party code that the notices do not list. The `.deb`, `.rpm`, `.app` and `.dmg` hold the app, its icons, its `.desktop` file or `Info.plist`, and the notices: `tauri.conf.json` sets no license file, EULA, changelog, frameworks or DMG background. The Windows installer downloads the WebView2 bootstrapper from Microsoft at install time and does not carry it. The compiler's runtime in the Windows executable has the entry above, since the owner's decision of 2026-11-05 (section 8). The Universal C Runtime that the app imports is part of Windows and has none (3.6 item 4).
   - In the web file only, with `{emsdk}` the version in `scripts/emsdk-version.sh`: `Emscripten runtime` `{emsdk}` (the JavaScript of `core/partsmith_core.mjs` and the system libraries in `core/partsmith_core.wasm`), `MIT OR NCSA`, `https://emscripten.org`, text `ui/scripts/notices/emscripten-{emsdk}/LICENSE`; `musl libc, in the Emscripten runtime` with no version, `MIT`, `https://musl.libc.org`, text `ui/scripts/notices/emscripten-{emsdk}/musl-COPYRIGHT`; and `LLVM libc++, libc++abi, libunwind and compiler-rt, in the Emscripten runtime` with no version, `Apache-2.0 WITH LLVM-exception AND (NCSA OR MIT)`, `https://llvm.org`, texts `ui/scripts/notices/emscripten-{emsdk}/libcxx-LICENSE.TXT`, `libcxxabi-LICENSE.TXT`, `libunwind-LICENSE.TXT` and `compiler-rt-LICENSE.TXT`. Each LLVM file holds the Apache 2.0 text with the LLVM exception and the NCSA and MIT texts that still cover older code, which ask for their notice with binaries.

   The Emscripten files are copies of the SDK's `upstream/emscripten/LICENSE`, `upstream/emscripten/system/lib/libc/musl/COPYRIGHT` and `upstream/emscripten/system/lib/{libcxx,libcxxabi,libunwind,compiler-rt}/LICENSE.TXT`. A new Rust, Emscripten or `webview2-com-sys` version needs new copies, or the generator fails.
7. **Partsmith's own license**: the root `LICENSE`.
8. **Prebuilt libraries.** Every crate of input 1 is searched for regular files ending in `.lib`, `.a`, `.dll`, `.so` or `.dylib` below its folder, outside any folder named `tests` (`system-deps` keeps a test library in `src/tests/lib`). A crate that ships one and is not in `PREBUILT_LIBRARY_CRATES` fails the generator: a prebuilt library is under the license of whoever built it, which the crate's own license may not cover.

**Normalized text.** A license text is decoded as UTF-8, with U+FFFD for an invalid byte sequence. Then a leading U+FEFF is removed, CRLF and a lone CR become LF, form feeds are removed, spaces and tabs at the end of each line are removed, blank lines at the start and the end are removed, and one LF ends the text. Two texts are the same text when their normalized forms are equal. Nothing else changes: the texts keep every other character as their packages ship it, including the em dash that AGENTS.md's style rules keep out of the project's own text (the owner accepted that exception on 2026-11-04, ADR 0033 way 1; the root AGENTS.md's rule has named it since 2026-11-06, the owner's decision of 2026-11-05, section 8).

**Layout.** In this order, with `{...}` filled in and the blank lines shown:

```text
{heading}

Generated by pnpm -C ui notices from the lock files (docs/spec/delivery.md section 2.9). Do not edit it by hand.

Partsmith is licensed under the Apache License 2.0, text [1]. Models you make with Partsmith belong to you. {scope} also contains the software listed below, each part under its own license; the license texts follow the list.

== Open CASCADE Technology ==

{OCCT statement}

{one entry line per OCCT entry}

== Fonts ==

{one entry line per font}

== Rust crates ==

Each crate's source code is at its crates.io URL; for the crates under MPL-2.0 that is where to obtain their Source Code Form.

{one entry line per crate}

== npm packages ==

{one entry line per package}

== Other libraries ==

{one entry line per library}

== License texts ==

[1]

{text 1}

[2]

{text 2}
```

- `{heading}` is `DESKTOP_NOTICES_HEADING` or `WEB_NOTICES_HEADING`, and `{scope}` is `The Partsmith desktop app for Linux, Windows and macOS` or `The Partsmith web app`.
- An entry line is `{name}`, then ` {version}` when it has one, then `, {license}, {url}`; then `, only {platforms}` for a desktop entry that some platform lacks (`{platforms}` the platforms that hold it, in the order of `DESKTOP_TARGETS`, joined by ` and `); then `, standard text` or `, text from its repository` when its `textSource` is `standard` or `repository`; then `, texts` followed by ` [{n}]` for each of its texts. `{license}` is the package's license field as written (`MIT/Apache-2.0` stays as it is), or `see texts` when it has none. `{url}` is `https://crates.io/crates/{name}/{version}` for a crate, `https://www.npmjs.com/package/{name}/v/{version}` for an npm package, and the URL of inputs 4 to 6 for the others.
- The OCCT entries are, first, `Open CASCADE Technology {OCCT_VERSION}, LGPL-2.1-only WITH OCCT-exception-1.0, https://github.com/Open-Cascade-SAS/OCCT/tree/{OCCT_TAG}, texts [2] [3]`, then one line per element of `bundled.json`, in its order, whose URL is `https://github.com/Open-Cascade-SAS/OCCT/blob/{OCCT_TAG}/{path}`.
- Fonts and other libraries come in the order of inputs 5 and 6. Crates and npm packages are sorted by name (byte order) and then by version precedence (Semantic Versioning 2.0.0).
- Texts are numbered from 1 in the order the file first refers to them, reading from the top, so `LICENSE` is always [1], OCCT's license and exception are [2] and [3], and the notices of `bundled.json` follow. Each distinct text appears once, as a line `[{n}]`, a blank line and the normalized text, with one blank line before the next `[{n}]`; the file ends with the last text's final LF.
- The paragraphs Partsmith writes itself are wrapped: the paragraph that starts `Generated by`, the one that starts `Partsmith is licensed`, each paragraph of `{OCCT statement}` and the one that starts `Each crate's source code`. The layout shows each on one line. The file holds the lines that `wrapParagraph` gives for it, with its commands marked (Wrapping below).

**Wrapping** (ui.md open issue 44, option (iii), decided by the owner on 2026-11-04). `wrapParagraph` splits a paragraph into words at its spaces and fills lines in order. A line takes the next word when the line, one space and the word together are at most 80 characters long. Otherwise the word starts a new line. A word longer than 80 characters gets a line of its own and is never split. A no-break space (U+00A0) is not a split point: the words it joins count as one word, and the line holds a space in its place. Every line is then at most 80 characters long, unless it is one longer word. A paragraph of 80 characters or fewer stays as it is. Joining the lines with single spaces gives the paragraph back, with a space for each no-break space. The same paragraph always gives the same lines.

Each shell command in these paragraphs stays on one line, where a reader can copy it whole. The generator joins the words of each command with no-break spaces. The commands are `pnpm -C ui notices` in the paragraph that starts `Generated by`, and these in the third paragraph of the OCCT statement: `mkdir -p <work>/src &&` and `tar -xzf occt-{OCCT_VERSION}-source.tar.gz --strip-components=1 -C <work>/src` in both files; `OCCT_WORK_DIR=<work> scripts/build-occt.sh native <prefix>`, `pnpm -C ui build` and `cargo tauri build` in the desktop file; and `git merge --no-ff`, `git log -1 --format=%T`, `OCCT_WORK_DIR=<work> scripts/build-occt.sh wasm <prefix>` and `pnpm -C ui build` in the web file. In the fourth paragraph they are `pnpm -C ui install --frozen-lockfile --registry http://127.0.0.1:8080/` in both files; `sha256sum -c SHA256SUMS --ignore-missing`, `cat rust-crates-desktop.tar.gz.part-* > rust-crates-desktop.tar.gz`, `cargo install tauri-cli --version {tauriCli} --locked`, `python3 -m http.server --bind 127.0.0.1 8080 --directory npm-packages-desktop` and `cargo tauri build` in the desktop file; and `sha256sum -c SHA256SUMS`, `cat npm-packages-web.tar.part-* > npm-packages-web.tar` and `python3 -m http.server --bind 127.0.0.1 8080 --directory npm-packages-web` in the web file. The `&&` stays with the `mkdir` command. A line may end after it, and a shell reads on to the next line. A command of one word, such as `scripts/build-wasm.sh`, needs no mark. With OCCT 7.9.3 every line of these paragraphs is at most 80 characters long. The longest command, the desktop file's `python3` command, is 77 characters long, and 78 with the comma after it.

Nothing else in the file is wrapped. The headings and the `[{n}]` lines are short. Each entry stays on one line. E-164 and test 40 read an entry as one line. The name, version and license field of most entries come from their packages. The license texts keep the lines their packages and repositories ship (Normalized text). In the web file of 2026-11-04, the longest entry line has 171 characters, and 31 lines of the license texts are longer than 200.

**The OCCT statement.** `{OCCT_VERSION}`, `{OCCT_TAG}`, `{repository}` and, in the desktop file, `{tauriCli}` (the `tauriCli` of `WINDOWS_INSTALLER`, input 6) come from the inputs; the rest is literal. Each paragraph is one line here, and the files hold it wrapped (Wrapping above). In the desktop file:

```text
The Partsmith desktop app uses Open CASCADE Technology (OCCT) {OCCT_VERSION}, https://dev.opencascade.org, copyright OPEN CASCADE SAS and the OCCT contributors. OCCT is free software under the GNU Lesser General Public License version 2.1 with the Open CASCADE exception version 1.0. It includes code from the other projects listed after it, under their own notices. All these texts are below.

OCCT is linked statically into the app. It is the unmodified source of the tag {OCCT_TAG} of https://github.com/Open-Cascade-SAS/OCCT, built as static libraries by the script scripts/build-occt.sh in the Partsmith source, which lists the toolkits and options used.

You may modify OCCT and use your version with Partsmith. The source of each release is on its page under {repository}/releases: the Partsmith source is the release's tag, v followed by the app's version (for example v0.1.0), with every script, tool version and lock file the build uses, also as the release's asset partsmith-source.tar.gz, which scripts/partsmith-source-archive.sh makes from that tag; the OCCT source the build reads is the release's asset occt-{OCCT_VERSION}-source.tar.gz, which scripts/occt-source-archive.sh makes from the tag {OCCT_TAG}. To relink: put your OCCT source in <work>/src (from the asset: mkdir -p <work>/src && tar -xzf occt-{OCCT_VERSION}-source.tar.gz --strip-components=1 -C <work>/src), run OCCT_WORK_DIR=<work> scripts/build-occt.sh native <prefix>, and build the app with OCCT_ROOT=<prefix> as README.md describes (pnpm -C ui build, then cargo tauri build in desktop/src-tauri). The exact commands for each platform, including the environment Windows and macOS need, are the steps of the bundle job in .github/workflows/release.yml at that tag.

The release also carries the source of every Rust crate and npm package the app is built from on Linux x86_64 with glibc, Windows x86_64 and macOS arm64, and of tauri-cli {tauriCli} and the crates of its own lock file, in the archives rust-crates-desktop.tar.gz and npm-packages-desktop.tar, which scripts/dependency-source-archive.mjs at that tag makes from Cargo.lock, ui/pnpm-lock.yaml and the Cargo.lock of tauri-cli. The second archive also holds the folder npm-sources-desktop, with the source of the code under MPL-2.0 in its native packages: lightningcss at the tag of each of its versions, with the crates its Node module compiles in, and the crates under MPL-2.0 that other native packages compile in. It also holds the license notices of the crates compiled into native packages whose tarballs do not carry them. Each archive is in the assets that end in .part-00, .part-01 and so on, which SHA256SUMS covers. Download them and SHA256SUMS, check them with sha256sum -c SHA256SUMS --ignore-missing, join the parts of each archive in order, for example cat rust-crates-desktop.tar.gz.part-* > rust-crates-desktop.tar.gz, and extract it. The archives take the place of crates.io and the npm registry, with the tools of README.md installed: set CARGO_HOME to the full path of the folder rust-crates-desktop that the first archive holds, install tauri-cli with cargo install tauri-cli --version {tauriCli} --locked, serve the folder npm-packages-desktop with python3 -m http.server --bind 127.0.0.1 8080 --directory npm-packages-desktop, and install the packages with pnpm -C ui install --frozen-lockfile --registry http://127.0.0.1:8080/ before the build. On Windows, cargo tauri build still downloads NSIS and Tauri's NSIS plugin for the installer.
```

In the web file:

```text
The Partsmith web app uses Open CASCADE Technology (OCCT) {OCCT_VERSION}, https://dev.opencascade.org, copyright OPEN CASCADE SAS and the OCCT contributors. OCCT is free software under the GNU Lesser General Public License version 2.1 with the Open CASCADE exception version 1.0. It includes code from the other projects listed after it, under their own notices. All these texts are below.

OCCT is linked statically into the app's geometry core, core/partsmith_core.wasm. It is the unmodified source of the tag {OCCT_TAG} of https://github.com/Open-Cascade-SAS/OCCT, built as static WebAssembly libraries by the script scripts/build-occt.sh in the Partsmith source, which lists the toolkits and options used.

You may modify OCCT and use your version with Partsmith. Each deployment of this site names the commit of {repository} it was built from in /SOURCE.txt, with a link to that commit's source archive; that commit holds every script, tool version and lock file the build uses. Each deployment holds that commit's source too, at /partsmith-source.tar.gz, which scripts/partsmith-source-archive.sh at that commit makes. Production is built from the main branch, and a preview from the commit GitHub makes by merging its pull request into the base branch; for a preview, /SOURCE.txt also names the two commits that merge joins and its tree: if the merge commit is gone, check out the first, run git merge --no-ff with the second, and compare the tree that git log -1 --format=%T prints with that tree. Each deployment also holds the OCCT source the build reads, at /occt-{OCCT_VERSION}-source.tar.gz. The script scripts/occt-source-archive.sh at that commit makes that file from the tag {OCCT_TAG}. To relink: put your OCCT source in <work>/src (from the archive: mkdir -p <work>/src && tar -xzf occt-{OCCT_VERSION}-source.tar.gz --strip-components=1 -C <work>/src), run OCCT_WORK_DIR=<work> scripts/build-occt.sh wasm <prefix> with the Emscripten SDK of scripts/emsdk-version.sh active, and build the core with OCCT_ROOT_WASM32_UNKNOWN_EMSCRIPTEN=<prefix> and then the app as README.md describes (scripts/build-wasm.sh, then pnpm -C ui build). The exact commands are the steps of the core-wasm and web jobs in .github/workflows/ci.yml at that commit.

Each deployment also holds the source of every Rust crate and npm package the app is built from on Linux x86_64 with glibc, where the web job builds it, in the archives rust-crates-web.tar.gz and npm-packages-web.tar, which scripts/dependency-source-archive.mjs at that commit makes from Cargo.lock and ui/pnpm-lock.yaml. The second archive also holds the folder npm-sources-web, with the source of the code under MPL-2.0 in its native packages: lightningcss at the tag of each of its versions, with the crates its Node module compiles in, and the crates under MPL-2.0 that other native packages compile in. It also holds the license notices of the crates compiled into native packages whose tarballs do not carry them. The parts of the two archives, which end in .part-00, .part-01 and so on, are in /dependencies/, and /dependencies/SHA256SUMS lists each part with its SHA-256. Download SHA256SUMS and each part it lists, check them with sha256sum -c SHA256SUMS, join the parts of each archive in order, for example cat npm-packages-web.tar.part-* > npm-packages-web.tar, and extract it. The archives take the place of crates.io and the npm registry, with the tools of README.md installed: set CARGO_HOME to the full path of the folder rust-crates-web that the first archive holds, serve the folder npm-packages-web with python3 -m http.server --bind 127.0.0.1 8080 --directory npm-packages-web, and install the packages with pnpm -C ui install --frozen-lockfile --registry http://127.0.0.1:8080/ before the build.
```

**Kept current.** A pull request that changes `Cargo.lock`, a `Cargo.toml` (the root crates' `repository`), `ui/pnpm-lock.yaml`, `rust-toolchain.toml`, `scripts/occt-version.sh`, `scripts/emsdk-version.sh`, a font license text, `LICENSE`, `ui/scripts/notices.mjs`, `ui/scripts/check-licenses.mjs` or a file under `ui/scripts/notices/` runs `pnpm -C ui notices` and commits both files with the change; otherwise `check:notices` fails the `schema` job (2.4). A pull request that changes the toolkits or `-DUSE_*` options of `scripts/build-occt.sh`, or the toolkits of `crates/partsmith-occt-sys/build.rs`, repeats the search of input 4 and updates `bundled.json` with what it finds; no check catches that one, since the generator does not read those files. The statements name what 2.13 makes, `/SOURCE.txt`, `partsmith-source.tar.gz` and `occt-{OCCT_VERSION}-source.tar.gz` (in the web statement at their paths in each deployment, `/partsmith-source.tar.gz` and `/occt-{OCCT_VERSION}-source.tar.gz`), `scripts/partsmith-source-archive.sh` and `scripts/occt-source-archive.sh`. A change to one of those names changes the statements in the same pull request (test 57). The fourth paragraph of each statement names the dependency archives of 2.13: their names, the folder `npm-sources-{scope}` of the npm archive, `/dependencies/` and its `SHA256SUMS` in the web statement, the install command of `tauri-cli` in the desktop statement, and `scripts/dependency-source-archive.mjs` (test 95). A change of the `tauri-cli` version of 2.2 updates `WINDOWS_INSTALLER`, the copies of input 6 (test 96) and `TAURI_CLI` of 2.13 (test 112). `ui/AGENTS.md` lists `notices`, `check:notices` and `check:dist` among its commands. Since other changes read only the root `AGENTS.md`, its Commands block has the line `pnpm -C ui notices   # after changing Cargo.lock, a Cargo.toml, ui/pnpm-lock.yaml, rust-toolchain.toml or scripts/*-version.sh`. That file is the owner's. The line was added on 2026-11-04 with the owner's leave (section 8). The release asset is the file of the tagged commit, whose CI passed (`verify`, 2.5).

**Not covered**: the CLI and the Python wheel, which M1 does not distribute (1.2). The code the NSIS installer adds has entries since 2026-11-04, and the Visual C++ runtime in the Windows app since 2026-11-05 (input 6, open issue 10).

### 2.10 npm license check (`ui/scripts/check-licenses.mjs`)

An ES module with no dependencies beyond Node's standard library, usable as a library and as a command; like `check-dist.mjs`, it runs its command only when it is the script Node started. It holds the npm packages that ship to the license allow list that `cargo-deny` applies to crates, so the policy has one source, `deny.toml` (ADR 0033). The packages that ship are the production dependencies of `ui/package.json` and theirs, and the development dependencies whose code the build writes into `ui/dist`. `check:licenses` does not check the other development dependencies: they do not ship. The dependency source archives give out every npm package of their scope. Their script holds each one to the same list with `licenseAllowed` (2.13, Licenses of the npm packages).

```js
/**
 * The development dependencies whose code the build writes into `ui/dist`: Rolldown's CommonJS interop helpers at the
 * top of `assets/index-*.js`, Vite's module preload polyfill (test 32), and Tailwind's CSS with its license banner.
 */
export const EMITTED_BUILD_PACKAGES = ['rolldown', 'tailwindcss', 'vite']

/** The strings of the `allow` array of the `[licenses]` table of `denyToml` (the text of deny.toml), in order. */
export function allowedLicenses(denyToml)

/** Whether the SPDX license expression `expression` is satisfied by the identifiers `allowed` (below). */
export function licenseAllowed(expression, allowed)

/**
 * The packages of `listing`, the standard output of `pnpm licenses list --json` with or without `--prod`, as
 * `{ name, version, license, path }`, one per name and version, sorted by name and then by version precedence.
 */
export function productionPackages(listing)

/**
 * The packages that ship: `productionPackages(production)` and, from `productionPackages(all)`, every version of each
 * name of EMITTED_BUILD_PACKAGES, each name and version once, sorted as `productionPackages` sorts. `production` and
 * `all` are the standard outputs of `pnpm licenses list --prod --json` and `pnpm licenses list --json`. Throws an
 * `Error` with the emitted-package message of section 4 for the first name, in list order, that `all` lacks.
 */
export function shippedPackages(production, all)

/** One message (section 4) for each package whose license `licenseAllowed` rejects, in the order of `packages`. */
export function licenseProblems(packages, allowed)

/** Orders two strings by code units, the same in every locale; the notices sort paths and names with it. */
export function compareText(first, second)

/** Orders `{ name, version }` records by name (byte order) and then by version precedence; the notices sort crates with it. */
export function compareNameAndVersion(first, second)

/**
 * Runs `command` with `args` in `directory` and resolves to its standard output; rejects with an `Error` whose
 * `status` is the exit status (or why it did not start) and whose `stderr` is its standard error.
 */
export function runCommand(command, args, directory)

/** The command-failure message of section 4 for a rejection of `runCommand`, shared by both scripts. */
export function commandFailureMessage(command, args, error)
```

Command: `node scripts/check-licenses.mjs` in `ui/` (`ui/package.json` gains `"check:licenses": "node scripts/check-licenses.mjs"`). It reads `deny.toml` from the repository root, found from the module's location, runs `pnpm licenses list --prod --json` and `pnpm licenses list --json` in `ui/`, and checks `shippedPackages` of the two:

- Exit 0 and `{n} npm packages that ship, each under a license deny.toml allows.` when there is no problem.
- Exit 1 with every problem on standard error, one per line, or with the message of section 4 when `deny.toml` has no allow list, a package of `EMITTED_BUILD_PACKAGES` is not installed, or `pnpm` fails.
- Exit 2 with `usage: node scripts/check-licenses.mjs` when given any argument.

**Reading `deny.toml`.** The `[licenses]` table runs from its header line to the next table header. Its `allow` key holds one array of double-quoted strings, which may span lines, hold `#` comments and end with a trailing comma. Anything else (no `[licenses]` table, no `allow` key in it, an unterminated array, an element that is not a double-quoted string) throws an `Error` with the deny.toml message of section 4. The repository's list is `MIT`, `Apache-2.0`, `Apache-2.0 WITH LLVM-exception`, `BSD-2-Clause`, `BSD-3-Clause`, `ISC`, `Zlib`, `Unicode-3.0`, `CC0-1.0`, `MPL-2.0`, `BSL-1.0`, `MIT-0`, `Python-2.0` and `BlueOak-1.0.0`, in this order. The owner added the last three on 2026-11-05 for the npm packages of the dependency archives (ADR 0033's amendment of that date). With them, `cargo deny check licenses` still passes and warns that it met no crate under `Python-2.0` or `BlueOak-1.0.0`, as it already warned for `BSD-2-Clause` and `ISC` (`license-not-encountered`, measured with cargo-deny 0.20.2 on 2026-11-05).

**Expressions** (SPDX license expressions). Spaces and parentheses separate tokens: `(`, `)`, the operators `AND`, `OR` and `WITH` (upper case only), and identifiers made of letters, digits, `.`, `-` and `+`. `WITH` binds tighter than `AND`, and `AND` tighter than `OR`; parentheses group. An identifier is satisfied when it equals an element of `allowed`, ignoring case; `A WITH B` when the text `A WITH B` equals an element, ignoring case (only `Apache-2.0 WITH LLVM-exception` today); `A AND B` when both are; `A OR B` when either is. Any other text is not satisfied: an empty license, `UNLICENSED`, `SEE LICENSE IN LICENSE.txt`, `Unknown` (what pnpm reports for a package without a license field), or an expression with a syntax error. The notices generator uses the same parser to find whether `Apache-2.0` or `MPL-2.0` alone satisfies an expression (2.9, input 3), by calling `licenseAllowed(expression, [identifier])`.

**Measured (2026-10-16).** Eight production packages: `react`, `react-dom`, `scheduler`, `zustand`, `clsx`, `@types/react` and `csstype` under MIT, and `lucide-react` under ISC; and three build packages whose code ships: `rolldown` 1.2.13, `tailwindcss` 4.3.3 and `vite` 8.3.4, all MIT. ADR 0033 logs the eleven. The other development dependencies include MIT-0, Python-2.0 and BlueOak-1.0.0, which do not ship. The list allows them since 2026-11-05. The dependency archives give them out (2.13).

### 2.11 Nightly workflow (`nightly.yml`)

Runs the sketch property tests (sketch.md 6.13, tests 189 to 196) with 4096 cases each, sixteen times the 256 that `cargo test` runs on every pull request, where the default keeps CI fast.

Triggers: `schedule` with the cron `23 4 * * *` (04:23 UTC, off the hour, when scheduled runs are delayed least) and `workflow_dispatch`. A scheduled run tests the head of `main`. Workflow `permissions: contents: read, actions: read`; `concurrency` `nightly-${{ github.ref }}` with `cancel-in-progress: false`; `env` `CARGO_TERM_COLOR: always`.

One job, `properties` (`name: Sketch property tests (4096 cases)`, `runs-on: ubuntu-24.04`, `timeout-minutes: 20`, so a property that never finishes ends after 20 minutes, not GitHub's default of 360), with these steps:

1. On `schedule` only, the skip check (`id: skip`, with `GH_TOKEN: ${{ github.token }}` and `GH_REPO: ${{ github.repository }}`, in the default shell `bash -e`): `gh run list --workflow nightly.yml --branch main --status completed --limit 1 --json headSha --jq '.[0].headSha // ""'` prints the commit of the newest finished nightly run on `main`, whatever its conclusion, or nothing when there is none. When it equals `$GITHUB_SHA`, the step writes `skip=true` to `$GITHUB_OUTPUT` and prints the skip notice of section 4; otherwise it writes `skip=false`. When `gh` fails, the step prints the skip-check warning of section 4 and writes `skip=false`, so the night tests: failing the step instead would record a failed run on that commit, which later nights would count as tested without a test having run. The running run has not finished, so it never counts. Every finished run counts: a skipped run concludes `success` on the same commit, so later nights keep skipping until `main` moves; a run that failed, timed out or was canceled is not repeated on the same commit, so a property that never finishes takes 20 minutes once, not every night (a `workflow_dispatch` run repeats any of them).
2. Only when `steps.skip.outputs.skip != 'true'`: `actions/checkout@v7.0.1`; `rustup show active-toolchain || rustup toolchain install`; `Swatinem/rust-cache@v2.9.2`; and `cargo test -p partsmith-sketch --release --test integration properties::` with `PROPTEST_CASES: "4096"` in its environment.

The crate is pure Rust, so the job needs no OCCT cache, no system packages and no Node. A failing property prints proptest's smallest failing case and its `cc` line; the fix commits that line to `crates/partsmith-sketch/tests/properties.proptest-regressions`, so every later `cargo test` replays it. A failure that sketch.md open issue 21 explains (a slide through a constraint that repeats others, which test 192 can still meet about once in 2,000 to 5,000 nightly runs) is the exception: its `cc` line is not committed. A committed line makes every later run replay the case and fail until the solver changes. The owner accepted that rate for M1 on 2026-11-04 and scheduled the fix, the exact trust-region step of ADR 0052 option (d), right after M1.

**Cost.** On 2026-10-16, on a 4-core machine, a release build of the sketch tests from a cold target took 2 min 26 s, the 4096-case run 1.6 s and the default 256-case run 0.1 s: the build is nearly all of the cost. With the job's Rust cache warm, a night after a change to `main` takes about 3 minutes of runner time (setup, cache restore, an incremental release build and the run), a night with a cold cache about 6, and a skipped night under 1, since the job stops after the skip check: about 90 minutes in a month with a change every day and about 30 in a quiet month. The repository is public, and standard GitHub-hosted runners are free for public repositories (ADR 0034), so none of it is billed; the skip still keeps the run from repeating work. The cron is the one place to change the cadence, for example to weekly with `23 4 * * 1`.

### 2.12 README.md

`README.md` is what a visitor reads first. It points at the specs for detail rather than repeating them, and it has these parts, in this order (m1-gap-list B-13):

1. **Title and summary**: the current heading and paragraph, unchanged.
2. **Status**: one line in place of "Status: early development (phase 0)": `> Status: milestone 1 in progress. What remains before the first release is in [the M1 gap list](docs/design/m1-gap-list.md).`
3. **Try it.**
   - The web app at `https://partsmith.pages.dev`, as a link, the production deployment of `main` (3.3). The owner confirmed that subdomain from the Cloudflare dashboard on 2026-11-04 (section 8). Pages fixes the subdomain when the project is created, adding a suffix to the project name when that name is taken, so it cannot be derived from `CLOUDFLARE_PAGES_PROJECT`. A new Pages project needs its address confirmed the same way. A pull request's preview is at `https://{branch}.partsmith.pages.dev` and is linked from the pull request. Then the supported browsers, as one sentence: the current Chrome, Edge, Firefox and Safari on desktop, at least Chrome and Edge 114, Firefox 128 and Safari 17 (ui.md 1.1), with every change tested in Chromium, and in Firefox and WebKit for the tests tagged `@engines` (2.14).
   - The desktop app: desktop builds appear on GitHub Releases, `https://github.com/blai30/partsmith/releases`, once the first release is published, which waits for the release checklist (3.6; until then every release is a draft, which only people with write access to the repository see, so the page holds nothing for visitors); then the files of desktop.md 3.8 and what each needs (Windows x64, where the installer fetches WebView2 when it is missing; macOS 13 or later on Apple silicon, with Safari 17 or later, whose engine the app draws with (desktop.md 1); Linux x86_64 with glibc 2.39 or later and WebKitGTK 4.1, such as Ubuntu 24.04), then the unsigned-bundle paragraph of 3.6 word for word, and `SHA256SUMS` to check a download (`sha256sum -c SHA256SUMS --ignore-missing`).
4. **Building.**
   - Prerequisites on Linux, the only platform every pull request builds the whole workspace on (CI also builds and tests the platform crates and the examples on Windows and macOS, 2.14, open issue 1): Git, CMake, Ninja and a C++17 compiler for OCCT; rustup, which installs the Rust of `rust-toolchain.toml` with its WebAssembly target (`rustup toolchain install` in the repository); Node 22 and pnpm 10.28.0 (`corepack enable && corepack install -g pnpm@10.28.0`: corepack takes the `packageManager` of `ui/package.json` only inside `ui/`, and from the repository root, where the `pnpm -C ui` commands run, it takes its own default instead, on 2026-10-16 pnpm 12.11.2, which the corepack of Node 22.22.0 fails to start); Python 3.12 or later for the Python package; and one install line, `sudo apt-get update && sudo apt-get install -y build-essential cmake ninja-build python3-venv libwebkit2gtk-4.1-dev libxdo-dev libssl-dev librsvg2-dev` (`python3-venv` gives Ubuntu's Python its `venv` module).
   - OCCT and the workspace: once, `sudo mkdir -p /opt/occt && sudo chown "$USER" /opt/occt`, because the build ends by installing into its prefix and `/opt` belongs to root (the prefix stays the `OCCT_ROOT` of AGENTS.md); then `scripts/build-occt.sh native /opt/occt/7.9.3/native`, `export OCCT_ROOT=/opt/occt/7.9.3/native`, `cargo test --workspace`.
   - The web core: the Emscripten SDK of `scripts/emsdk-version.sh` (4.0.15) installed and activated (in a clone of `https://github.com/emscripten-core/emsdk`, `./emsdk install 4.0.15 && ./emsdk activate 4.0.15 && source ./emsdk_env.sh`), `scripts/build-occt.sh wasm /opt/occt/7.9.3/wasm`, `export OCCT_ROOT_WASM32_UNKNOWN_EMSCRIPTEN=/opt/occt/7.9.3/wasm`, then `scripts/build-wasm.sh`, which writes `ui/public/core/`. Without it the web app opens but its geometry core does not load.
5. **Running**, one command block each:
   - The web app: `pnpm -C ui install`, `pnpm -C ui dev`, then `http://localhost:5173`; and the production build, `pnpm -C ui build`, which writes `ui/dist` (the web statement of 2.9 points here for relinking).
   - The desktop app: once, `cargo install tauri-cli --version 2.12.2 --locked`; then `pnpm -C ui dev`, and in a second terminal `cargo tauri dev` in `desktop/src-tauri`. A bundle: `pnpm -C ui build`, then `cargo tauri build` in `desktop/src-tauri`.
   - The CLI: `cargo run -p partsmith-cli -- <command>`, with its five commands `validate`, `fmt`, `rebuild`, `export` and `schema`, one line each as cli.md section 1 describes them.
   - Python: a virtual environment, `pip install maturin pytest`, `(cd python && maturin develop --release)`, then `python -m partsmith build script.py -o part.ptsm` (python-api.md 2.1 and 2.10).
6. **Contributing**: [AGENTS.md](AGENTS.md) (rules, commands and invariants), [the spec index](docs/spec/README.md), [the ADR log](docs/adr/README.md) and the [technical design](docs/design/technical-design.md); and the regeneration commands: examples, `PARTSMITH_BLESS=1 cargo test -p partsmith-cli --test integration examples::` and `PARTSMITH_BLESS=1 pytest python/tests/test_examples.py` (2.8); the documents the UI writes, `PARTSMITH_BLESS=1 pnpm -C ui e2e round-trip.spec.ts`, which needs the web core of `scripts/build-wasm.sh` (2.8, ui.md E-160); the engine's golden 3MF files, `PARTSMITH_BLESS=1 cargo test -p partsmith-engine --test integration export_and_import::t160` (engine.md 3.20); schemas, `cargo run -p partsmith-cli -- schema --write && pnpm -C ui generate:protocol` (3.4); notices, `pnpm -C ui notices` (2.9).
7. **License**: Partsmith is under the Apache License 2.0 ([LICENSE](LICENSE)), one license for the code, the specifications, the file format, the examples and the test corpus (ADR 0028), and models made with Partsmith belong to their makers. The third-party software and its licenses are in [THIRD-PARTY-NOTICES.txt](THIRD-PARTY-NOTICES.txt) for the desktop app and [ui/public/THIRD-PARTY-NOTICES.txt](ui/public/THIRD-PARTY-NOTICES.txt) for the web app. In place of README.md's current two OCCT sentences ("OCCT 7.9.3 is used ... with a modified OCCT."), the OCCT sentences are: `OCCT 7.9.3 is used under the LGPL 2.1 with the Open CASCADE exception. It is linked statically, and every build comes with a way to its source: each web deployment names the commit it was built from in /SOURCE.txt and holds that commit's source as /partsmith-source.tar.gz and OCCT's source as /occt-7.9.3-source.tar.gz, and each release carries partsmith-source.tar.gz and occt-7.9.3-source.tar.gz next to its tag (ADR 0034). Both also carry the source of every Rust crate and npm package the build uses: each web deployment in /dependencies/, for the build on Linux x86_64 that CI makes, and each release as the parts of rust-crates-desktop.tar.gz and npm-packages-desktop.tar. The notices say how to build Partsmith with a modified OCCT and without the network.` The file names may be set as code. The sentences follow ADR 0034 and 2.13, and change with them. There is no CC0 grant to scope: ADR 0028 replaced it.

The release checklist is not in `README.md`; it is in `.github/workflows/AGENTS.md` (3.6).

### 2.13 Source of each build (`SOURCE.txt`, `scripts/occt-source-archive.sh`, `scripts/partsmith-source-archive.sh`, `scripts/check-source-archive.sh`, `scripts/dependency-source-archive.mjs`)

Every build Partsmith distributes links OCCT statically, and ADR 0034 (option A, the owner's decision of 2026-10-16) is to meet LGPL 2.1 section 6 with published source. The repository `https://github.com/blai30/partsmith` is public, so every build's commit or tag, or for a preview its two parents, is there. This section adds what makes each build's source findable: a stamp in every web deployment that names its commit, and the OCCT source archive, written into every web deployment and attached to every release. On 2026-11-04 the owner took the strictest reading of LGPL 2.1 sections 4 and 6 for each of ADR 0034's four questions, with no lawyer. Every web deployment and every release then also carries the source of every Rust crate and npm package that the app's executables and the web build are compiled from (The dependency source archives, below). The code the Windows installer adds has entries in the desktop notices (2.9 input 6). On 2026-11-05 the owner decided what else the dependency archives carry (section 8). The desktop crate archive also holds `tauri-cli` 2.12.2 and the crates of its own lock file, with no toolchains attached. The npm archives also hold lightningcss's source at the tag of each locked version, with the crates its Node module compiles in, and the notices of the crates compiled into the native packages whose tarballs lack them. Every npm package of the archives passes the license check of 2.10. `libdbus-sys`'s `vendor/dbus/` leaves the crate archive (The dependency source archives, below). The statements of 2.9 name all of them. The owner also decided on 2026-11-05 that every deployment holds Partsmith's own source as `/partsmith-source.tar.gz`. Every release carries it too, by the owner's rule (Partsmith's source archive, below). The owner decided the same day that every CI, platforms and release run that uploads an artifact holding OCCT's compiled code also uploads the source of that artifact's scope, kept a day longer than the artifact (The source with every artifact, below). A local build carries none, since it is not distributed.

**The source stamp.** Every build the `web` job uploads as `web-dist`, and so every deployment, previews included, holds the regular file `SOURCE.txt` at its root, served at `/SOURCE.txt`. It is UTF-8 with LF line endings and one final newline, and holds these lines, in this order:

```text
Source of this deployment of the Partsmith web app
Repository: {repository}
Commit: {commit}
Source archive: {repository}/archive/{commit}.tar.gz
Source archive in this deployment: /partsmith-source.tar.gz
```

For a build of a pull request, three more lines follow:

```text
Pull request: {pull request}
Parents: {base} {head}
Tree: {tree}
```

- `{repository}` is `${{ github.server_url }}/${{ github.repository }}`, `https://github.com/blai30/partsmith`; the source archive is GitHub's `.tar.gz` of the commit.
- The fifth line names the deployment's own archive of the same commit (Partsmith's source archive, below). It stays with the deployment when GitHub drops a preview's merge commit, whose archive the fourth line links.
- `{commit}` is `$GITHUB_SHA`, the commit the run checked out and built, as 40 lowercase hexadecimal digits. On a push to `main` it is the pushed commit. On a pull request it is the merge commit GitHub makes for the pull request's checks (`refs/pull/{number}/merge`, the pull request's head merged into its base branch), which every job of the run checks out, builds and tests. Pages records the pull request's head commit for its dashboard instead (`--commit-hash`, 2.4); the stamp names what was built.
- `{pull request}` is `${{ github.event.pull_request.html_url }}`, and `{base}` and `{head}` are the two parents of `{commit}` in the order its commit object lists them: the base branch's commit, then the pull request's head. GitHub moves `refs/pull/{number}/merge` to a new merge commit whenever either side changes, so the merge commit of an older preview may no longer be on any ref and may be dropped in time, while its parents stay reachable: the base in its branch's history, the head through `refs/pull/{number}/head`, which GitHub keeps. From them the merge can be made again. `{tree}` is the merge commit's tree, so whoever makes the merge again can check that its tree is the one built.

The writer is the `web` job's step `Write the source stamp`, between the steps that run `pnpm build` and `pnpm check:dist`, in the job's working directory `ui/` and in GitHub's shell for a step without `shell`, `bash -e`. Its `env` holds `REPOSITORY_URL: ${{ github.server_url }}/${{ github.repository }}` and `PULL_REQUEST_URL: ${{ github.event.pull_request.html_url }}`, which is empty on a push; `GITHUB_SHA` is GitHub's. It writes the five lines to `dist/SOURCE.txt`, and when `PULL_REQUEST_URL` is not empty it appends the three pull request lines. The checkout is shallow, so `git log` and `git rev-parse` see no parents; the step reads the parents and the tree from the header of the commit object, the lines of `git cat-file commit "$GITHUB_SHA"` before the first blank line (the message below it may hold a line starting with `parent` or `tree`), and fails when that command fails. `check:dist` does not look for the stamp, so `pnpm build && pnpm check:dist` keeps working where nothing is deployed.

The check is the `deploy` job's step `Check the source stamp` (2.4), after `web-dist` is downloaded and before Wrangler runs: unless `ui/dist/SOURCE.txt` holds the whole line `Commit: $GITHUB_SHA`, it prints the source-stamp error of section 4 and fails the job, so no deployment goes out without a stamp that names the run's commit. The stamp and its check land in the same commit as the statements of 2.9 that name them, and so do the archive steps of the `sources` jobs below.

**The OCCT source archive.** `scripts/occt-source-archive.sh <directory>` writes `<directory>/occt-{OCCT_VERSION}-source.tar.gz`, the OCCT source that `scripts/build-occt.sh` reads, from the tag that `scripts/occt-version.sh` pins (`{OCCT_VERSION}` and `{OCCT_TAG}` come from that file, as in 2.9). Like `build-occt.sh`, it runs with `set -euo pipefail` and sources `occt-version.sh` from its own folder.

- **Clone.** It reads the OCCT clone at `$OCCT_WORK_DIR/src`, with `OCCT_WORK_DIR` and its default (`${TMPDIR:-/tmp}/occt-{OCCT_VERSION}`) as in `build-occt.sh`. When that folder does not exist, it first clones it exactly as `build-occt.sh` does, `git clone --depth 1 --branch {OCCT_TAG} https://github.com/Open-Cascade-SAS/OCCT.git "$OCCT_WORK_DIR/src"`, so after a local OCCT build it reuses that build's clone.
- **Pinned commit.** `scripts/occt-version.sh` also sets `OCCT_COMMIT=a016080bf6738d6aeae020badee4e888ad1540a5`, the commit the tag names (measured below). Before archiving, the script checks that `git -C "$OCCT_WORK_DIR/src" rev-parse "$OCCT_TAG^{commit}"` prints `OCCT_COMMIT`, and `scripts/build-occt.sh` makes the same check right after its own clone (not when `<work>/src` already exists, which is how a modified OCCT is built). Each exits 1 with the tag-commit message of section 4 otherwise, so a tag moved upstream stops the archive and a cold OCCT build instead of making them from a source other than that of the cached builds; each first removes the clone it made, so that the next run of either clones and checks again instead of building or archiving from it (tests 55 and 58). The change that adds the pin changes the OCCT cache keys (3.2), so OCCT is built once more for each key; it changes no notices text.
- **Contents.** The tree of the tag `{OCCT_TAG}` in that clone, never its working tree or another commit, limited to `.clang-format`, `CMakeLists.txt`, `LICENSE_LGPL_21.txt`, `OCCT_LGPL_EXCEPTION.txt`, `README.md`, `adm` and `src`, all under the folder `occt-{OCCT_VERSION}/`. With the options of `build-occt.sh`, CMake reads `CMakeLists.txt`, `adm` (its modules and templates) and `src` (the sources, the file lists of each toolkit and package, and the resources), copies `.clang-format` (its configure step fails without it), and installs the two license files; `README.md` is upstream's description of the source. The rest of the tag (`data`, `dox`, `samples`, `tests`, `tools`, `.github` and the other dot files at its root) is read only with options that `build-occt.sh` turns off.
- **Bytes.** `git -C "$OCCT_WORK_DIR/src" -c core.autocrlf=false -c core.eol=lf -c tar.umask=0002 -c core.attributesFile=/dev/null archive --format=tar --prefix=occt-{OCCT_VERSION}/ {OCCT_TAG} -- .clang-format CMakeLists.txt LICENSE_LGPL_21.txt OCCT_LGPL_EXCEPTION.txt README.md adm src`, piped to `gzip -9 -n`. The four `-c` options fix what a user's or the system's Git configuration would otherwise change: the line endings of files without an `eol` attribute (`core.autocrlf`, and `core.eol` for a file whose attributes set `text` or `text=auto`; OCCT's `.gitattributes` sets `eol` only for some patterns, and `adm/cmake/*.cmake` has none), the file modes (`tar.umask`), and attributes such as `export-ignore` from a global attributes file (`core.attributesFile`, whose default, `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/git/attributes` or `~/.config/git/attributes`, is read even when the option is unset). `git archive` of a tag gives every entry the tagged commit's time and stores the commit's id in the archive's pax header (`git get-tar-commit-id` reads it back), and `-n` keeps the name and the time out of the gzip header, so the same tag gives the same bytes on every run with the same `git` and `gzip`, whatever the Git configuration and the global attributes file say: anyone can make the archive again from the pin and compare. A system attributes file (`$(prefix)/etc/gitattributes`) would still apply. The script does not turn it off. A change of the script changes the key of the archive's cache and asks for test 56 again. The step `Write the OCCT source archive` warns when the bytes change (The archive in every deployment, Copied into the build).
- **Output.** It writes to a temporary file in `<directory>` and renames it to the archive's name when `gzip` has finished; on any failure an `EXIT` trap removes the temporary file, so a failure leaves `<directory>` as it was. Then it prints `Wrote {directory}/occt-{OCCT_VERSION}-source.tar.gz from {OCCT_TAG}, commit {commit}.` (`{directory}` as given, `{commit}` the tagged commit's full id) and exits 0.
- **Failures.** With other than one argument, it exits 2 with `usage: scripts/occt-source-archive.sh <directory>` on standard error. When `$OCCT_WORK_DIR/src` holds no tag `{OCCT_TAG}`, or is not a Git clone (such as an extracted archive), it exits 1 with the no-tag message of section 4; when the tag names another commit than `OCCT_COMMIT`, it exits 1 with the tag-commit message. Any other failure, such as a failed clone, a missing `<directory>` or a listed path missing from the tag (a pin whose OCCT drops or renames one), exits with a status other than 0: `pipefail` makes the failure of `git archive` fail the pipeline, whose `gzip` alone would end with 0.

Every release carries the archive as an asset next to `SHA256SUMS`, which covers it. `release.yml`'s `sources` job makes it with this script in every run and puts it into the artifact `sources-desktop`. `draft` downloads that artifact into `release/` (2.5, The source with every artifact). The script needs only `git` and `gzip`, which the runner has. Its clone took 14 s on the runner (`occt-native` of CI run 41926888431, 2026-11-04). The archive comes from the OCCT pin of the commit the run checked out, in a tag run the tagged commit, the same pin the `bundle` jobs build OCCT from (3.2).

**Measured** (2026-10-16, the tag `V7_9_3`, commit `a016080bf6738d6aeae020badee4e888ad1540a5`, git 2.43.0 and gzip 1.12): `occt-7.9.3-source.tar.gz` holds 15,712 entries (15,224 files and 488 folders), 120,401,920 bytes as tar, and is 17,457,358 bytes (16.6 MiB) with SHA-256 `bb71bf7473f9a278648f3ff96e28bd50afe49bf8d6e53ab55e883b54d0220155`, the same on two runs and from a fresh clone of the upstream tag, which named the pinned commit. Without the `-c` options, `core.autocrlf=true` (the default of Git for Windows), `tar.umask=0077` and a global attributes file holding `*.cmake export-ignore` each change the bytes; with them, each of the three gives that SHA-256 again. `core.eol=crlf` changes nothing for 7.9.3: each pattern of OCCT's `.gitattributes` that makes a file text also sets its `eol`. The script has set `core.eol=lf` since 2026-11-05 all the same, for a later pin. With it the archive of 7.9.3 is the same 17,457,358 bytes with the same SHA-256 (measured on 2026-11-05 from a fresh clone of the tag). The build of test 56 from that archive stands. The whole tag, packed the same way, is 48,068,564 bytes. CMake's configure step, with the toolkits and options of `build-occt.sh`, succeeds on the extracted archive; without `.clang-format` it fails (`file COPY cannot find .../.clang-format`, `CMakeLists.txt` line 771), which is why the file is in the list. The full build from the archive is test 56.

**That the build works from the archive alone** (ADR 0034) is test 56, by hand: when the archive step is first implemented, and again in every pull request that changes `scripts/occt-source-archive.sh`, `scripts/build-occt.sh` or `scripts/occt-version.sh`, which records the result. No CI check catches a file the build needs and the archive lacks, since CI builds OCCT from a clone (3.2). Tests 55 and 58 replace the clone with a stub. The real clone runs in CI whenever the archive's cache misses (The archive in every deployment, The source with every artifact).

**The archive in every deployment** (the owner's decision of 2026-11-04, ADR 0034, open issue 15). Every build the `web` job uploads as `web-dist` holds the regular file `occt-{OCCT_VERSION}-source.tar.gz` at its root, next to `SOURCE.txt`. Every deployment holds it too, previews included. Pages serves it at `/occt-{OCCT_VERSION}-source.tar.gz`. It is the file `scripts/occt-source-archive.sh` makes from the OCCT pin of the commit the run built. The desktop bundles never carry it: their `ui/dist` comes from a `pnpm -C ui build` of their own, and only the `web` job copies the archive into a build. The archive, its check and the web statement that names its path land in one commit.

- **Made in the `sources` jobs.** Right after its checkout, the `sources` job of `ci.yml` (The source with every artifact, below) has a step with `id: occt-source` that uses `actions/cache@v6.1.0` with `path: .occt/source` and `key: occt-source-${{ hashFiles('scripts/occt-source-archive.sh', 'scripts/occt-version.sh') }}`. It restores the entry when there is one. On a miss, the next step, with `if: steps.occt-source.outputs.cache-hit != 'true'` and GitHub's default shell `bash -e`, runs these two lines:

  ```sh
  mkdir -p .occt/source
  scripts/occt-source-archive.sh .occt/source
  ```

  The cache's post step then saves `.occt/source` when the job passes. A change of the pin misses this cache. The archive script then clones the tag and checks its commit (Clone and Pinned commit, above). A run that also misses the OCCT native cache clones the tag a second time, in `occt-native`'s `scripts/build-occt.sh`, in a job that runs beside `sources` (14 s for the clone on the runner, `occt-native` of CI run 41926888431). A failure of the step fails `sources` and the jobs that need it (First in the run, below), and no other: in `ci.yml`, `rust`, `schema`, `python` and `desktop` keep running. Such a failure is likely only in a pull request that changes `scripts/occt-source-archive.sh` or the pin, or when upstream moves the tag or drops a listed path (Failures, above). `release.yml`'s `sources` job has no cache step (ADR 0034's amendment of 2026-11-05, choice 5). Right after its checkout, a step with no `if` runs the same two lines in every release run, dry runs included. The archive script clones the tag and checks its commit (Clone and Pinned commit, above), as `draft` did in every tag run until wave G. A release then attaches the archive its own run made from upstream's tag, never a cache entry another run wrote. A restored entry could only be checked by the commit id its tar names. A SHA-256 check would fail a release when the runner's `git` or `gzip` alone changed the bytes. The two lines took 19 s here (the clone took 14 s on the runner, in `occt-native` of CI run 41926888431). A release run takes about 20 s longer. Until wave G, `occt-native` made the archive in two steps before its OCCT build. `web`, which needed it, restored the entry. Wave G moved the two steps into the `sources` jobs (ADR 0034's amendment of 2026-11-05, choice 1). `occt-native` makes no archive. Its OCCT build no longer waits for one.
- **Copied into the build.** Since 2026-11-05 the archive reaches the build through the run's source artifact (The source with every artifact, below). Its step `Write the OCCT source archive` in each `sources` job comes after the two steps of Made in the `sources` jobs (above) and before `Write the Partsmith source archive`. It runs in the repository root and sets no `shell`. Its `env` holds only `OCCT_SOURCE_SHA256: bb71bf7473f9a278648f3ff96e28bd50afe49bf8d6e53ab55e883b54d0220155`, the SHA-256 that Measured (above) records for the pinned tag and commit. It runs these lines:

  ```sh
  source scripts/occt-version.sh
  archive="occt-$OCCT_VERSION-source.tar.gz"
  mkdir sources
  cp ".occt/source/$archive" sources/
  if ! scripts/check-source-archive.sh "sources/$archive" "$OCCT_COMMIT"; then
    echo "::error title=OCCT source missing::sources/$archive from the cache .occt/source is not a whole gzip file or does not hold the source of the OCCT commit $OCCT_COMMIT, so nothing was uploaded. Delete that cache entry (gh cache list --key occt-source-) and run the job again, which makes the archive anew (docs/spec/delivery.md section 2.13)."
    exit 1
  fi
  sha256="$(sha256sum "sources/$archive" | cut -d ' ' -f 1)"
  echo "$sha256  $archive"
  if [ "$sha256" != "$OCCT_SOURCE_SHA256" ]; then
    echo "::warning title=OCCT source archive changed::$archive has the SHA-256 $sha256, not $OCCT_SOURCE_SHA256, the one docs/spec/delivery.md section 2.13 records. A new pin, git or gzip wrote other bytes: record the new SHA-256 there and in OCCT_SOURCE_SHA256 of each step Write the OCCT source archive."
  fi
  ```

  `cp` fails the step when the archive of the checked-out pin is missing. The step then checks the copy with `scripts/check-source-archive.sh`, as `deploy` checks the build's (The archive check, below). Unless it is a whole gzip file whose tar names the commit `OCCT_COMMIT`, the step prints the cached-archive error of section 4 and fails before the upload. The archive script checked the commit when it made the entry (Pinned commit, above). The step checks again what the run uploads: an entry of the cache is checked by nothing else before a CI run uploads it. `deploy` checks deployments only. In `release.yml` the folder holds the archive the job has just made. Every run that passes the check then prints the archive's SHA-256 in the line format of `sha256sum`. The log of each run names the bytes its source artifact and its deployment carry. `sha256sum -c` checks a downloaded copy against that line. When the SHA-256 differs from `OCCT_SOURCE_SHA256`, the step prints the warning of section 4 and goes on. The archive still holds the pinned source, which the step has just checked and `deploy` checks again. Its bytes depend on the runner's `git` and `gzip` (Bytes, above), which GitHub updates. A failure would stop every deployment for a change of bytes alone. A change of the pin fails test 86 until Measured names the new tag and commit and `OCCT_SOURCE_SHA256` holds the SHA-256 that Measured records. `web` downloads the source artifact into `ui/dist`. `check:dist` then holds the archive to the 25 MiB limit like every other file (3.5). `check:dist` does not look for the archive, as it does not look for the stamp. `pnpm build && pnpm check:dist` keeps working where nothing is deployed.
- **Checked before the deploy.** The `deploy` job's step `Check the OCCT source archive` (2.4) comes after `Check the source stamp` and before Wrangler. It sources `scripts/occt-version.sh` of its checkout. Unless `scripts/check-source-archive.sh ui/dist/occt-{OCCT_VERSION}-source.tar.gz "$OCCT_COMMIT"` passes, that is unless the file is a whole gzip file whose tar names the commit `OCCT_COMMIT` (The archive check, below), the step prints the OCCT-source error of section 4 and fails the job. No deployment then goes out without OCCT's source, or with the archive of another pin or a damaged one.
- **The same bytes.** The cache holds the bytes the archive step made. Deployments carry that same file until the key changes or GitHub drops the entry (7 days unused, or the least recently used beyond the repository's 10 GB, 3.2). A pull request's run that finds no entry for its key, in its own cache scope or its base branch's, makes one in its own scope, which `main` never reads. That pull request's previews carry its file. A new entry is made with the runner's `git` and `gzip` of that day. The bytes depend on `git` and `gzip` (Bytes, above). With git 2.43.0 and gzip 1.12 they are those of Measured above, SHA-256 `bb71bf7473f9a278648f3ff96e28bd50afe49bf8d6e53ab55e883b54d0220155`, made again on 2026-11-04 from a local clone of the tag. The `ubuntu-24.04` runner had git 2.56.0 on 2026-11-04 (the `web` job of CI run 41785469932) and gzip 1.12, Ubuntu 24.04's. It writes the same bytes: the first `main` run that made the entry, CI run 41926888431 (`0ae7337`, 2026-11-04), cloned the tag with git 2.56.0, and `web` printed that SHA-256 (open issue 15). Every `sources` job prints the SHA-256 of the archive and warns when it is not that of Measured (Copied into the build, above). Open issue 15 records the SHA-256 of the pull request run and of the first `main` run that make the entry.
- **Size and upload.** At 17,457,358 bytes for 7.9.3, the archive is 8,757,042 bytes under the 25 MiB limit. `web-dist` grows by its 17.5 MB. A later pin whose archive is over the limit fails `check:dist` in `web`. Like the core, it then needs an ADR (3.5): the archive split into parts under the limit that the statement names, or another place the owner controls. Wrangler 4.149.0 sends the hash of every file of a deployment to Pages (`/pages/assets/check-missing`) and uploads only the files whose hash Pages reports missing (`upload` in its `src/pages/upload.ts`). The hash is BLAKE3 over the file's bytes in base64 followed by its extension (`hashFile` in `src/deploy/helpers/hash.ts` of `@cloudflare/deploy-helpers`, which Wrangler bundles and its `src/pages/hash.ts` re-exports). The same archive always gives the same hash. The log line of the upload counts the files Wrangler skipped: `Uploaded 7 files (9 already uploaded)` in the deploy job of CI run 41785469932. After the upload, Wrangler sends the hashes of every file of the deployment to `/pages/assets/upsert-hashes`. When that call fails, Wrangler warns that the next deployment may have to upload the files again: Pages can drop a hash. Wrangler skips the archive's upload while Pages still holds its hash.
