It gates nothing: no job of `ci.yml` or `release.yml` waits for it and none of its jobs is in `deploy.needs`. A release waits for a passing run of it on its commit (3.6).

`platforms.yml`'s triggers: `workflow_dispatch` and `schedule` with the cron `41 5 * * 1` (Mondays at 05:41 UTC, off the hour), never `push`, `pull_request` or `workflow_call`. A scheduled run tests the head of `main`; a dispatched run tests the ref it was started from, and only runs on `main` count for promotion and for a release (3.6). There is no skip check like the nightly one (2.11): a week without a change to `main` still runs, because the runner image and the toolchain on it change from week to week. Workflow `permissions: contents: read`; `concurrency` `platforms-${{ github.ref }}` with `cancel-in-progress: false`, so a second dispatch waits for the running one instead of canceling an OCCT build before it saves its cache; `env` the three entries of `ci.yml`'s (`CARGO_TERM_COLOR: always`, `OCCT_ROOT: ${{ github.workspace }}/.occt/native`, `CARGO_PROFILE_DEV_DEBUG: "0"`).

The jobs of `ci.yml` (2.4), after `desktop` and before `deploy`, in this order:

| Job (name) | Needs | Runs on | What it runs |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `occt-platforms` (`OCCT (${{ matrix.runner }})`) | | matrix `runner`: `windows-2025`, `macos-15` | Checkout; OCCT native in `.occt/native` with `actions/cache@v6.1.0` (`id: occt`, as in the `bundle` job) and the key of 3.2, which is the `bundle` job's; on a miss (`steps.occt.outputs.cache-hit != 'true'`) the compiler setup of 3.6 step 1 for its runner (Windows: `ilammy/msvc-dev-cmd@v1.13.0`; macOS: the step `Install Ninja and set the deployment target` of `release.yml`, which runs `brew install ninja` and writes `MACOSX_DEPLOYMENT_TARGET=13.0` to `$GITHUB_ENV`), then `scripts/build-occt.sh native "$OCCT_ROOT"` with `OCCT_JOBS: 4` (3.6 step 2); and last the step `Disk space after the build`, `df -h .` with `if: always()`, since a cold OCCT build is the largest user of the runner's disk. |
| `rust-platforms` (`Rust tests (${{ matrix.runner }})`) | `occt-platforms`, with `if: ${{ !cancelled() }}` | the same matrix | Checkout; on Windows `ilammy/msvc-dev-cmd@v1.13.0` and the step `Use the MSVC linker for Rust` of `release.yml`; on macOS a step `Set the deployment target` that writes `MACOSX_DEPLOYMENT_TARGET=13.0` to `$GITHUB_ENV`, so the shim and the test binaries are built for the macOS that OCCT and the app are built for; `.occt/native` restored with `actions/cache/restore@v6.1.0`, the same key and `fail-on-cache-miss: true`; `rustup show active-toolchain \|\| rustup toolchain install`; `Swatinem/rust-cache@v2.9.2` with `save-if: ${{ github.ref == 'refs/heads/main' }}` and `cache-on-failure: true`, since its post step otherwise saves only after a job that passed, and each run while a failure is being fixed then restores the dependency builds of the one before (3.2); the step with `id: crates` and `timeout-minutes: 20` that runs `cargo test --no-fail-fast -p partsmith-occt-sys -p partsmith-engine -p partsmith-desktop`, so one failing test binary does not hide the others, and a test binary that hangs ends that step and the examples runner and clippy still run (the first run's Windows tests hung until the job's 60 minutes, open issue 1 (g)); the examples runner, `cargo test -p partsmith-cli --test integration examples::`, with `if: ${{ !cancelled() && steps.crates.outcome != 'skipped' }}`, so it runs after failing crate tests too; then `cargo clippy -p partsmith-occt-sys -p partsmith-engine -p partsmith-desktop --all-targets -- -D warnings` with the same `if`, because CI's workspace clippy runs on Linux only and never sees code behind `#[cfg(windows)]` or `#[cfg(target_os = "macos")]`, such as desktop.md tests 68 to 70; on Windows, the step `Check the imports of the app` of 3.6 (The C runtime on Windows), which runs `scripts/check-windows-imports.sh target/debug/partsmith-desktop.exe` on the app that Cargo builds in the `crates` step for the desktop crate's integration tests, with `if: ${{ !cancelled() && steps.crates.outcome != 'skipped' && runner.os == 'Windows' }}`; on Windows, right after it, the step `Check the imports of the CLI`, which runs `scripts/check-windows-imports.sh target/debug/partsmith.exe` on the CLI that Cargo builds for the examples runner, with the same `if`; and last the step `Disk space after the tests`, `df -h .` with `if: always()`. |
| `e2e-engines` (`End-to-end tests (${{ matrix.browser }})`) | `core-wasm` | `ubuntu-24.04`, with the matrix `browser`: `firefox`, `webkit` | `ci.yml`'s `e2e` for one engine, in `ui/`: `core-wasm` downloaded into `ui/public/`, pnpm and Node 22, `pnpm install --frozen-lockfile`, the step `Restore the apt archives of ${{ matrix.browser }}`, on a hit the step `Copy the apt archives into apt's cache` (3.2), the step `Install ${{ matrix.browser }} and its system packages`, which runs `pnpm exec playwright install --with-deps ${{ matrix.browser }}` with `timeout-minutes: 10` (below), on `main` the step `Copy the apt archives out of apt's cache` and, when the install downloaded a package the entry lacked, the step `Save the apt archives of ${{ matrix.browser }}` (3.2), then `xvfb-run --auto-servernum pnpm e2e --project ${{ matrix.browser }}`, which selects that engine's project (Playwright runs it only when `--project` names it) and runs the tests tagged `@engines` in it (ui.md 6.8), on a virtual display, since the `firefox` project runs headed; `webkit` runs headless, as in `e2e`, and ignores the display. `xvfb-run` is part of the `xvfb` package, which the runner image holds and `--with-deps` updates; its default screen, 1280 by 1024 at 24 bits, holds the 1280 by 720 viewport with the window around it. With `if: failure()` it uploads `ui/playwright-report/` and `ui/test-results/` as `playwright-report-${{ matrix.browser }}` (`retention-days: 7`). |

The jobs of `platforms.yml`, in this order:

| Job (name) | Needs | Runs on | What it runs |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `occt-platforms` (`OCCT for the full rebuild (${{ matrix.runner }})`) | | matrix `runner`: `macos-15` | `ci.yml`'s `occt-platforms`, line for line, but for its `name` and its matrix, which holds the one row that `full-rebuild` needs (promotion step 1). Its Windows step never runs. Its name keeps its check apart from `ci.yml`'s `OCCT (macos-15)`, which branch protection requires (The move, choice 3). |
| `full-rebuild` (`Full rebuild of bench50 (macos-15)`) | `occt-platforms`, with `if: ${{ !cancelled() }}` | `macos-15` | Exactly these steps, in this order: checkout; `.occt/native` restored with `actions/cache/restore@v6.1.0`, the key of `occt-platforms` and `fail-on-cache-miss: true`; `rustup show active-toolchain \|\| rustup toolchain install`; `Swatinem/rust-cache@v2.9.2` with the two inputs of `ci.yml`'s `rust-platforms`; and the step `Time the full rebuild of bench50`, with `OPERATION: run of bench50 from an empty cache` as its one `env` entry (the full-rebuild gate, below). The job's `env` is `MACOSX_DEPLOYMENT_TARGET: "13.0"`, the macOS that OCCT and the app are built for. |

Every matrix sets `fail-fast: false`, so a failure on one runner or in one engine cancels nothing else. A job's `needs` waits for every row of the matrix it names, so without its `if: ${{ !cancelled() }}` one runner's failed OCCT build would skip the Rust tests on both runners; with it, both rows of `rust-platforms` run once both OCCT rows have ended, each restores the OCCT of its own OS, and only a row whose OCCT is missing fails, at its `fail-on-cache-miss`. `full-rebuild` has the same `if` (ADR 0054 decision 2). With the one row of its `occt-platforms`, it still measures after a copy that failed with its OCCT already saved (a failed checkout, say). After a failed cold build it fails at its restore, whose message names the missing cache, instead of being skipped. On a cold run, each row of `rust-platforms` waits for the slower of the two OCCT builds. `occt-platforms` stays a job of its own, rather than steps of `rust-platforms` as in `bundle`, because `actions/cache` saves in its post step only when its job succeeds: in one job, a failing test would throw away the cold OCCT build that ran before it, and every run until the tests pass would build OCCT again. `occt-platforms`, `rust-platforms` and `full-rebuild` set `defaults.run.shell: bash` (Git Bash on Windows), as `bundle` does, and `e2e-engines` sets `defaults.run.working-directory: ui`, as `e2e` does. `timeout-minutes` is 150 for `occt-platforms` in both files, which may build OCCT, and 60 for `rust-platforms`, `e2e-engines` and `full-rebuild`; the `crates` step has 20 of its own, and `e2e-engines`' browser install 10. A step past its timeout fails without canceling its job. The steps after `crates` whose `if` holds `!cancelled() && steps.crates.outcome != 'skipped'` still run; 20 minutes leave a wide margin over the first run's crate tests (2 min 34 s on macOS with their build, and a 3 min 23 s build on Windows, open issue 16). The browser install's time is the Ubuntu mirror's: `--with-deps` downloads the engine's system packages with apt (about 50 MB for Firefox and 117 MB for WebKit on `ubuntu-24.04`) from the first mirror of the runner's apt list, whose speed differed by region and by day. 14 of the first 16 installs took 15 s to 2 min 47 s, and the third run's two took 21 min 17 s and 53 min 17 s, its WebKit row ending 5 minutes short of the job's 60 (open issue 16). Since wave H2 the install finds those packages in apt's cache when the apt cache of 3.2 hits. On a hit apt downloads only the packages Ubuntu updated since the newest save and those the entry lacks. `apt-get update` still fetches the package lists from the mirror. Since wave H3 a run on `main` saves those downloads in a new entry (3.2). The whole set comes from the mirror only on a miss: a run before `main` has saved any entry for the browser, a run after GitHub dropped every entry for it or a person deleted them, and a run whose restore failed (3.2). With 10 minutes, a mirror that slow ends the install step, which GitHub reports as timed out under the step's name (`Install webkit and its system packages`), so the row fails at about 11 minutes instead of running into the job's timeout. The tests after it are skipped, and the report upload, which runs on `if: failure()` with `actions/upload-artifact`'s default `if-no-files-found` (`warn`), finds neither folder and adds the warning `No files were found with the provided path` next to the timeout error, as in `ci.yml`'s `e2e` after a failed install. Such a failure is the mirror's, not a defect of the app or a test: it is recorded in open issue 16, and `gh run rerun <run-id> --failed` runs the failed rows again on new runners, with `deploy` after them, within 12 hours of the end of the run's `sources` job. Later, `gh run rerun <run-id>` runs the whole run again, `sources` included (2.13, Kept a day longer). A CI run is rerun only while it is the newest run of its concurrency group (2.4): on `main`, the run that `gh run list --workflow ci.yml --branch main --event push --limit 1` lists; on a pull request, a run whose head commit (`gh run view <run-id> --json headSha`) is the pull request's head (`gh pr view <number> --json headRefOid`). `--branch` alone also lists the runs of a fork's pull request whose branch has the same name. A rerun joins the concurrency group of 2.4 and cancels a newer run in progress. On `main`, its `deploy` would put the older commit into production after the newer one (3.3). When a newer run exists, its result stands (The move, choice 2). No step sets `continue-on-error`.

**Results.** The expected results are the same on every platform: the examples runner compares the Windows and macOS rebuilds with the expected files blessed on Linux, at the tolerance of 2.8, and no example has expected results of its own per platform. A build or test that fails on one platform or in one engine, or a result outside the tolerance, is a defect: it is fixed in the code, or in a test that relied on something only Linux or Chromium does, without weakening what the test checks there. Until it is fixed, it is recorded with the job, the test and what was found in open issue 1 (Windows and macOS) or in ui.md open issue 41 (an engine). No test is allowed to fail on one platform to make a run pass. A test leaves the engine subset only through a change to ui.md 6.8 that names the engine feature it lacks, with an open issue; never through `test.skip`, `test.fixme` or a condition on the engine or the platform in the test. An engine that gets no WebGL2 on the runner for a reason of the runner's own (no display, or Firefox's startup graphics probe past its 4-second wait, ui.md 6.8) is neither a defect of the app nor of a test: that project gets the option that fixes it (headed on the virtual display `e2e-engines` gives every run, or a browser preference, ui.md 6.8), and the change is recorded in ui.md open issue 41; its tests are never left out for it. Firefox showed that page on the first run. If headed Firefox still gets no WebGL2 context and E-147's message (ui.md 6.5), which carries the context creation error, names EGL or the GL driver, the `firefox` row installs `libegl1` and `libegl-mesa0` before its browser, the two packages that the first run's `webkit` row got from `--with-deps webkit` and its `firefox` row lacked, with this section, 3.2 and tests 62 and 130 changed with it, and those packages go through the apt cache of 3.2; until a run shows that, the row installs nothing more. Headed under `xvfb-run`, Firefox passed all 24 on the second run with nothing more installed.

**The full-rebuild gate** (technical design section 14, ADR 0054). The owner decided on 2026-11-04 that the desktop budget of a full rebuild of a 50-feature part is judged on the macOS arm64 runner of this workflow, `macos-15`. The budget was 1 s. On 2026-11-05 the owner set it from the runner's first median, 1.477 s, plus 20 percent for runner noise: 1.8 s ([ADR 0057](../adr/0057-budgets-set-from-the-measured-median-plus-20-percent.md)). The benchmark row holds it. `full-rebuild` times it with the existing benchmark of recompute.md section 5. Its last step runs `cargo bench -p partsmith-recompute --bench budgets -- "$OPERATION"`, which builds the benchmark in the `bench` profile (the `release` profile with its thin LTO, as the app is built) and measures only the row `run of bench50 from an empty cache`: one untimed run of `bench50` that checks no feature fails, then the median of 7 runs, each on a new `Recompute`. The step prints what the benchmark prints and reads the row that `Report` of `partsmith-budgets` writes for the operation: the line that starts with the operation and two spaces, whose last three fields are the median, the word `budget` and the budget. It then appends to `$GITHUB_STEP_SUMMARY`:

```markdown
## Full rebuild of bench50

| Runner | Operation | Median | Budget |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| {RUNNER_OS} {RUNNER_ARCH} | run of bench50 from an empty cache | {median} | {budget} |

{verdict}
```

`{median}` and `{budget}` are as the row writes them, such as `1.780s` and `1.800s`. `{verdict}` is `Within the budget of technical design section 14.`, or, when the benchmark also printed its line `Over budget: {operation} (`, `Over the budget of technical design section 14. The owner decides with an ADR that raises the budget or schedules speed work (ADR 0054).`, and the step then prints the warning of section 4. Either way the step exits 0. The benchmark exits 1 over its budget, and Cargo then prints `error: bench failed` on standard error. The step does not use that status, since the number is the owner's call and not a failed run. When the benchmark prints no row for the operation (the build failed, the benchmark panicked, or `bench50` is missing and the benchmark printed `Not measured:`), the step writes nothing to the summary, prints the error of section 4 and exits 1.

The job gates nothing and is never promoted (below). It measures one number on one runner for the owner. No Linux job of `ci.yml` can measure it there. Its Rust cache is its own, release builds kept apart from the debug builds of `rust-platforms` (3.2). A release waits for a passing platforms run of its commit (3.6). That run's summary holds the commit's median. The checklist's platforms item asks for that median within its budget, or for an ADR the owner decided that covers it (3.6, the owner's decision of 2026-11-05, section 8). Measured here on 2026-11-04, on the 4-core Linux container: a cold release build of the benchmark took 1 min 32 s (74 crates), and 30 s with every dependency built, the state a warm Rust cache leaves, since rust-cache keeps no workspace crate. The step's run of the benchmark took 14 to 16 s, with medians of 1.780, 1.892 and 1.676 s. The step's script, run here on the real benchmark, read 1.821 s and wrote the summary with the over-budget verdict. The dependency builds that rust-cache keeps took about 170 MB on disk. The job runs after the copy of `occt-platforms`' `macos-15` row, the run's only other job. In platforms run 15 it took 2 min 45 s. The first runs record its times, cold and warm, its cache's size and the median on `macos-15` (test 106, open issue 16, recompute.md open issue 4).

**Promotion.** A job other than `full-rebuild` and the copy of `occt-platforms`' `macos-15` row that it needs (step 1), or one row of its matrix, moves into `ci.yml` once it has passed in the two latest runs of `platforms.yml` on `main` (`gh run list --workflow platforms.yml --branch main`, .github/workflows/AGENTS.md). A row of `e2e-engines` that failed at its browser install's timeout, the one failure that is not fixed in the code or a test (above), counts only once rerun with `gh run rerun <run-id> --failed`, or with `gh run rerun <run-id>` more than 12 hours after the run's `sources` job ended (2.13, Kept a day longer): the rerun attempt's result is then the run's, as `gh run list` reports it, and a run not rerun counts as failed for that row. The pull request that moves a job:

1. moves the job into `ci.yml` as it is, keeping only the rows that passed (the others stay in `platforms.yml`), with the jobs it needs: `occt-platforms` moves with `rust-platforms`, and `e2e-engines` then uses `ci.yml`'s own `core-wasm`, so `platforms.yml` drops its `occt-wasm`, `sources` and `core-wasm` with it. Whenever `occt-platforms`, or its `macos-15` row, moves, `platforms.yml` keeps a copy of the `macos-15` row, whatever else is left there: `full-rebuild` needs it. A `needs` that names a job the file no longer has makes GitHub reject the whole workflow (test 104);
2. adds the job to `deploy.needs` (2.4), so it gates merges and deploys like every other CI job;
3. updates 2.4, this section, tests 49, 59, 60 to 64, 85, 86, 93, 100, 102, 104 and 118, every text that names the moved job or the workflow (1 and 1.1, 2.2 with its actions table and runners line, the `platforms.yml` row of 2.13's table in The source with every artifact, 3.2, 3.6 checklist item 2 and its copy in `.github/workflows/AGENTS.md`, ui.md 1.1 with its Tested in column and 6.8, desktop.md 6) and, with the times of the pull request's own CI run, section 5: a job that makes CI's wall time longer than its budget moves only with a change of that budget, which a new ADR sets by ADR 0057's method under the owner's rule of 2026-11-05 (the measured time plus a stated margin for runner noise);
4. once `full-rebuild` and its `macos-15` row of `occt-platforms` are all that is left in `platforms.yml`, keeps the workflow and checklist item 2's platforms clause, whose run then holds the full-rebuild median of the commit to release.

The owner then adds the job to the checks that branch protection requires (section 8).

**The move** (wave H, ADR 0038's amendment of 2026-11-06, Accepted (owner, 2026-11-05), G14). Every job and row of `platforms.yml` but `full-rebuild` passed in platforms runs 14 (41926898084, on 0ae7337) and 15 (42156838426, on a3df7f5), the two latest runs on `main`, both after wave F and each with no rerun. All three jobs moved with both rows, as steps 1 to 4 say: `occt-platforms` with `rust-platforms`, and `e2e-engines` with `ci.yml`'s own `core-wasm` in place of `platforms.yml`'s. `platforms.yml` dropped its `occt-wasm`, `sources` and `core-wasm`. It kept `full-rebuild`, the copy of `occt-platforms`' `macos-15` row, its triggers, permissions, concurrency and `env`, and the platforms item of the release checklist (3.6). Since wave H no job of `platforms.yml` is left to promote. The move reached `main` with no pull request: `main` was fast-forwarded after the local checks passed. The pull request's own CI run of step 3 is then the first CI run on `main` after the move (test 129).

- **Where the jobs go.** In `ci.yml` the three jobs come after `desktop` and before `deploy`, in their order in `platforms.yml`. `deploy.needs` names them after `desktop`, in the same order (2.4). `occt-platforms` is named there although `rust-platforms` needs it. `rust-platforms` runs after a failed OCCT row (its `if` holds `!cancelled()`). `deploy` then does not depend on how GitHub reads the `needs` of the jobs before it.
- **What stays.** The moved jobs keep their ids, names, step names and cache keys. The copy keeps its id `occt-platforms` and takes the name of choice 3. `Swatinem/rust-cache` builds its key from the job id, the runner's OS and architecture, a hash of the environment and a hash of `Cargo.lock`, each `Cargo.toml`, `.cargo/config.toml` and `rust-toolchain.toml`, with no workflow name (in run 15, `v0-rust-rust-platforms-Darwin-arm64-2943b290-70a3b689` on `macos-15`). The environment hash covers the `rustc -vV` of every Rust toolchain installed on the runner and each environment variable whose name starts with `CARGO`, `CC`, `CFLAGS`, `CXX`, `CMAKE` or `RUST`. A row's key changes with any of those files, and also with a runner image that has another installed Rust toolchain or another MSVC. The Windows row's `CARGO_TARGET_X86_64_PC_WINDOWS_MSVC_LINKER` is the path of MSVC's `link.exe`. That path names the MSVC version. The `macos-15` row's key changed with its runner image once, between platforms runs 7 and 8 (open issue 16). `ci.yml`'s `env` is `platforms.yml`'s (test 60). The OCCT and Rust caches that platforms runs saved on `main` then hit in `ci.yml`. No OCCT is built again. Every job that uploads an artifact still reaches `sources` through its `needs` (test 118): `e2e-engines` needs `core-wasm`, which needs `sources`.
- **Concurrency** (choice 1, Accepted (owner's rule of 2026-11-05)). `ci.yml` keeps `cancel-in-progress: true` (2.4). A newer run of the same ref cancels a run whose OCCT build has not ended, and the build is lost. `actions/cache` saves only after its job passes. That is why `platforms.yml` keeps `false`. In `ci.yml`, `occt-native` and `occt-wasm` already build under this rule. The longest cold build of `occt-platforms`, 1 h 10 min 21 s on Windows in platforms run 14 (open issue 1 (e)), is shorter than `occt-wasm`'s 88 minutes. A cold build happens only when an OCCT script changes or GitHub dropped a cache. The next run of the ref builds it again and saves it, on runners that bill nothing here. One risk is new to `ci.yml`: `rust-platforms` is its one job with `cache-on-failure: true`. rust-cache v2.9.2 runs its post step on `success() || env.CACHE_ON_FAILURE == 'true'`, with no check for a canceled job. It saves when its key missed. A newer push that cancels the job while it builds after a key miss (a change to `Cargo.lock`, a `Cargo.toml`, `.cargo/config.toml` or the toolchain, or a runner image with another installed Rust toolchain or another MSVC) then saves a partial Rust cache. rust-cache saves only on a key miss. Every later run builds what that cache lacks until the key changes. The first platforms run saved such a cache on Windows after its timeout (open issue 16). The job stays as it is (G14). The remedy is `gh cache delete <key>` on the partial entry (`gh cache list --key v0-rust-rust-platforms-` lists it). The next passing run on `main` then saves a full one. Open issue 16 records each case. Options: (a) keep `true` for every run (chosen); (b) `false` for every run, where each run of a ref waits for the one before; (c) `${{ github.event_name == 'pull_request' }}`, which cancels superseded pull request runs and lets every run on `main` finish. The rule picks (a). It is the setting GitHub's documentation gives for superseded runs and the one CI already holds for its own OCCT builds. (b) and (c) change how every CI job and the deploy run on `main`. The move does not need that.
- **Reruns** (choice 2, Accepted (owner's rule of 2026-11-05)). A CI run is rerun only while it is the newest run of its concurrency group (above). Options: (a) that rule (chosen); (b) a step in `deploy` that skips the deploy when the run's commit is no longer the head of its branch; (c) no rule. The rule picks (a), the safest: it leaves `deploy`, the job that publishes, as it is. A person who reruns a run checks one command first. (b) adds a GitHub API or git call with the run's token to that job. An outage of that call would block every deploy. It would also change the exact `deploy` YAML of 2.4 and tests 53, 87, 93 and 120. A rerun of CI's own `e2e` had the same risk before the move. With `e2e-engines`' install timeout in CI, reruns come more often.
- **Pull requests.** The three jobs read no secret and need none. On a pull request from a fork, which gets no secrets and a read-only `GITHUB_TOKEN`, they run as on any other pull request. GitHub-hosted Windows and macOS runners bill nothing for this public repository (ADR 0034). `rust-platforms` saves its Rust cache on `main` alone (`save-if`): a pull request restores `main`'s and saves none. `occt-platforms` saves an OCCT install under the pull request's ref only on a miss, when the pull request changes an OCCT script, as `occt-native` and `occt-wasm` do (3.6, Caches after the static runtime).
- **Timeouts.** They stay: 150 minutes for `occt-platforms`, 60 for `rust-platforms` and `e2e-engines`, 20 for the `crates` step and 10 for the browser install. In CI an install that ends at its timeout fails its row and skips `deploy` until the row is rerun (above). CI's `e2e` kept its Chromium install with no timeout of its own until wave H2. Wave H2 gave it `e2e-engines`' step, with the same 10 minutes and the apt cache of 3.2 (choice 7).
- **Wall time** (section 5). The platform jobs run beside `core-wasm` and `e2e`. They end before `e2e` in every warm run measured. Platforms run 15 and CI run 28 give the estimate. `occt-platforms` took 15 s on `macos-15` and 21 s on `windows-2025` in run 15. `rust-platforms` starts when both rows have ended. It took 6 min 6 s on `windows-2025` and 4 min 15 s on `macos-15`. It ends about 7 minutes after the push on Windows and about 5 minutes after it on macOS, plus the waits for a macOS runner (5 minutes before run 15's `rust-platforms`). `e2e-engines` starts when `core-wasm` ends, 7 min 18 s after the push in CI run 28. It took 1 min 53 s (Firefox) and 2 min 16 s (WebKit). It ends about 9.5 minutes after the push. `e2e` starts at the same time. It took 8 min 54 s on a fast apt mirror (run 41785469932) and 16 min 4 s on CI run 28's slow one (open issue 16). It ends about 16 or about 23.5 minutes after the push. The critical path stays `sources`, `core-wasm`, `e2e` and `deploy`. CI's wall time stays about 17 minutes on a fast mirror. It stays about 24 minutes when only `e2e`'s runner gets a mirror as slow as run 28's. Both are within section 5's 25 minutes. The budget stays. An `e2e-engines` row on such a mirror fails. At run 28's 48.6 kB/s, Firefox's 48.8 MB take about 17 minutes and WebKit's 117 MB about 40. The install ends at its 10-minute timeout. The row fails about 19 minutes after the push. `deploy` then waits for a person's rerun. The time from push to deploy goes past 25 minutes. Section 5 does not count such a run (choice 4). Since wave H2 a mirror that slow sends a browser install its whole package set only when the apt cache misses. On a hit it still serves the package lists and the packages updated since the save (3.2, choice 7). Two other things can make a platform job the last before `deploy`: a wait for the macOS OCCT row of more than about 9.5 minutes (both rows of `rust-platforms` wait for it, and the Windows row then ends last), or waits for the two macOS jobs of more than about 11 minutes in all. The first CI run on `main` after the move measures the wall time. Its time replaces this estimate in section 5 (test 129).
- **The budget and a failed install** (choice 4, Accepted (owner's rule of 2026-11-05)). Section 5's CI budget leaves out a run whose browser install ended at its timeout, as it leaves out a cold OCCT build. Such a run fails. Open issue 16 records its rerun. Options: (a) that exclusion (chosen); (b) a CI budget that covers a failed install and its rerun; (c) `e2e-engines` out of `deploy.needs`, which no longer holds the deploy, or its install without a timeout, which runs on into the job's 60 minutes. The rule picks (a). A wall-time budget measures runs that pass, as section 5 already does for a cold OCCT build. (b) would set the budget by a person's response time. (c) goes against G14 and promotion steps 1 and 2: the jobs move as they are and join `deploy.needs`.
- **Runner time.** Each CI run gains about 15 minutes of runner time: run 15's `rust-platforms` (6 min 6 s and 4 min 15 s) and `e2e-engines` (1 min 53 s and 2 min 16 s), and about half a minute of `occt-platforms`. A platforms run drops to the copy of the macOS OCCT row and `full-rebuild`, about 3 minutes (run 15's `full-rebuild` took 2 min 45 s). None of it is billed.
- **Caches** (3.2). CI restores the Windows and macOS OCCT installs and the Rust caches of `rust-platforms` in every run and keeps them in use. The weekly platforms run restores the macOS OCCT install alone. A week without a CI run leaves the Windows one unused. GitHub can drop it after 7 days. The next CI run then builds it cold within the 150 minutes of `occt-platforms`, outside section 5's budget, as it does a cold `occt-native`. A newer push that cancels `rust-platforms` while it builds after a key miss can leave a partial Rust cache on `main` (choice 1). Under GitHub's 10 GB, the caches dropped first are now `full-rebuild`'s Rust cache and the `bundle` job's Rust caches, which only the platforms run and the release use (3.2).
- **The release.** The platforms item of the release checklist stays as it was, word for word in both copies (3.6, tests 82 and 83). CI on the commit to release now holds the Windows, macOS and engine jobs. The platforms run on it holds the full-rebuild median (step 4).
- **The copy's name** (choice 3, Accepted (owner's rule of 2026-11-05)). The copy of the `macos-15` row is named `OCCT for the full rebuild (${{ matrix.runner }})`. GitHub matches a required check by its name, whatever workflow reports it. A platforms run dispatched on a pull request's branch would otherwise report a second `OCCT (macos-15)` on the pull request's head commit. Options: (a) `ci.yml`'s name, for a copy line for line; (b) a name of its own, with the copy two lines off `ci.yml`'s job (chosen); (c) `runs-on: macos-15` with no matrix and no Windows step. The rule picks (b): each required check then has one source, the common practice for required checks. Test 128 compares the copy with `ci.yml`'s job but for those two lines. (c) would leave more lines that the test cannot compare.
- **Required checks.** The owner adds the six checks of the moved jobs to the checks that branch protection requires (section 8): `OCCT (windows-2025)`, `OCCT (macos-15)`, `Rust tests (windows-2025)`, `Rust tests (macos-15)`, `End-to-end tests (firefox)` and `End-to-end tests (webkit)`.
- **`.github/workflows/AGENTS.md`** (G14's leave). The move changes only the sentences of that file that name a moved job or say which workflow runs it. Every other sentence stays byte for byte, and so does the platforms item of the release checklist (tests 82 and 83). The changes:
  - Interface, the `ci.yml` line: its jobs end "`desktop`, `occt-platforms`, `rust-platforms`, `e2e-engines`, then `deploy`", and its artifacts end "`playwright-report` on an `e2e` failure, and `playwright-report-<browser>` on an `e2e-engines` failure".
  - Interface, a new line after the `python` line: the descriptions of the three jobs that the `platforms.yml` line held, word for word, from "`occt-platforms` restores or builds OCCT on `windows-2025` and `macos-15`" to "or no `api-ms-win-crt` DLL (delivery.md 3.6);", then from "`e2e-engines` installs its engine" to "`xvfb-run --auto-servernum pnpm e2e --project ${{ matrix.browser }}`.".
  - Interface, the `platforms.yml` line: its triggers and its sentence on the gate as they are, then "`occt-platforms` (`OCCT for the full rebuild (macos-15)`) is a copy of `ci.yml`'s `macos-15` row;", the `full-rebuild` clause as it is, ending in a period, and "It uploads no artifact." The clauses on `sources`, `occt-wasm`, `core-wasm` and the artifacts go.
  - Rules, the OCCT builders: "only `ci.yml`'s `occt-native`, `occt-wasm` and `occt-platforms`, `platforms.yml`'s copy of `occt-platforms`, and `release.yml`'s `bundle` job build it".
  - Rules, before each tag: "The weekly platforms run restores the Windows and macOS OCCT caches too, which usually keeps them alive (delivery.md 3.2)." becomes "CI's `occt-platforms` restores the Windows and macOS OCCT caches on every run, and the weekly platforms run the macOS one, which usually keeps them alive (delivery.md 3.2)."
  - Rules, promotion: "keeps a copy of it for `full-rebuild`" ends ", and the copy is never promoted"; after "Run 14 (41926898084, on 0ae7337) was the first (ADR 0038).", the sentence "Run 15 (42156838426, on a3df7f5) was the second, and `occt-platforms`, `rust-platforms` and `e2e-engines` moved into `ci.yml` with both rows in wave H (delivery.md 2.14, The move)."; "A failure in `platforms.yml`" becomes "A failure of `occt-platforms`, `rust-platforms`, `e2e-engines` or `full-rebuild`"; and "before the run counts for promotion" becomes "while the run is the newest of its concurrency group in `ci.yml` (delivery.md 2.14)".
  - Rules, Git Bash: "release and platforms steps" becomes "release and CI steps".
- **Other texts.** The texts outside the specs that named the platforms workflow for the moved jobs name CI: README.md's Try it and Building (2.12), the `pnpm e2e --project firefox` line of `ui/AGENTS.md` ("as CI's `e2e-engines` job runs them"), the `@engines` entry of `ui/e2e/README.md` ("as CI's `e2e-engines` job runs it") and the comment on tests 68 to 70 in `desktop/src-tauri/AGENTS.md` ("CI's rust-platforms job runs them on windows-2025"). `crates/partsmith-budgets/AGENTS.md` and `crates/partsmith-recompute/AGENTS.md` name `full-rebuild` in `platforms.yml`, where it stays.

**Cost.** The repository is public, so standard runners, Windows and macOS included, bill nothing (ADR 0034). The second run, with every cache warm except part of the Windows Rust cache (open issue 16), took 7 min 26 s of wall time, from dispatch to its last job finished, and 18 min 42 s of runner time, the sum of the times of its eight jobs counting each matrix row, against the hour and the one and a half to two hours ADR 0038 estimated; section 5 holds the wall-time budget. The first run also built OCCT on Windows and macOS within the 150 minutes of `occt-platforms`, and later runs restore it, as the release's `bundle` jobs then do. The cron is the one place to change the cadence. The third run, warm, took 1 h 0 min 22 s of wall time and 1 h 33 min 18 s of runner time, 1 h 14 min 34 s of it in its two browser installs, on a slow mirror that the install's timeout now cuts off at 10 minutes. The first run's times and cache sizes, and the third run's installs, are in open issue 16. Since the move, a platforms run is the copy of the macOS OCCT row and `full-rebuild`. The platform jobs add about 15 minutes of runner time to each CI run (The move).

## 3. Behavior

### 3.1 Job graph and gating

- A pull request merges only when every CI job passes (branch protection on `main` requiring every job of 2.4 except `deploy`; a repository setting, section 8), including `Source archives (web)` and the six checks of the platform jobs (2.14, The move). A failed `sources` skips `core-wasm`, `web`, `e2e`, `e2e-engines` and `deploy`. GitHub counts a skipped required check as passed.
- Jobs without `needs` start at once. OCCT builds run only on a cache miss (a change to `scripts/build-occt.sh`, `scripts/occt-version.sh` or, for WebAssembly, `scripts/emsdk-version.sh`), which takes tens of minutes; otherwise they restore in about a minute.
- `deploy` runs after everything else, so production only ever receives a build whose tests passed, and a preview exists for every pull request whose checks pass.
- `cancel-in-progress` drops superseded runs of the same ref; a canceled deploy leaves the previous deployment active, because Pages activates a deployment only after its upload completes. A rerun of an older run joins the same group and cancels a newer run in progress. A CI run is rerun only while it is the newest run of its concurrency group: on `main`, the run that `gh run list --workflow ci.yml --branch main --event push --limit 1` lists, and on a pull request, a run whose head commit is the pull request's head (`gh pr view <number> --json headRefOid`, 2.14).

### 3.2 Caches and system packages

| Cache | Key | Path |
| --- | --- | --- |
| OCCT native | `occt-native-${{ runner.os }}-${{ hashFiles('scripts/build-occt.sh', 'scripts/occt-version.sh') }}` (as today; on Windows and macOS the same formula gives per-OS keys) | `.occt/native` |
| OCCT WebAssembly | `occt-wasm-${{ hashFiles('scripts/build-occt.sh', 'scripts/occt-version.sh', 'scripts/emsdk-version.sh') }}` (as today) | `.occt/wasm` |
| OCCT source archive | `occt-source-${{ hashFiles('scripts/occt-source-archive.sh', 'scripts/occt-version.sh') }}`, restored by `ci.yml`'s `sources` job, which makes and saves it on a miss; `release.yml` uses no such cache (2.13) | `.occt/source` |
| Cargo | `Swatinem/rust-cache` defaults | registry and `target/` |
| pnpm store | `actions/setup-node` `cache: pnpm` on `ui/pnpm-lock.yaml` | pnpm store |
| `tauri-cli` | `tauri-cli-2.12.2-${{ runner.os }}-${{ runner.arch }}` | `~/.cargo/bin/cargo-tauri` (`cargo-tauri.exe` on Windows) |
| Apt archives of a job's system packages | `apt-archives-ubuntu-24.04-<name>-${{ github.run_id }}`, restored with `restore-keys` `apt-archives-ubuntu-24.04-<name>-`: the job's runner, the package set (`<name>` is `chromium` in `e2e`, `firefox` and `webkit` in `e2e-engines`, and `tauri` in `rust`, `desktop` and `release.yml`'s Linux `bundle` row) and the run that saved it; the newest entry of the set restored in every run, and a new one saved on `main` alone, when the install downloaded a package the entry lacked (below) | `~/apt-archives` |

Tag-triggered release runs restore caches saved on `main`; a run's own caches are scoped to its ref, so no later tag restores what a tag run saves, and GitHub drops caches unused for 7 days. CI saves the Linux, WebAssembly, Windows and macOS OCCT caches on `main`. Its `occt-platforms` job builds the Windows and macOS ones under the `bundle` job's keys and restores them in every run. That keeps them alive while `main` or a pull request changes at least once a week (2.14). The weekly platforms run restores the macOS one too. A release dry run (`workflow_dispatch`) started from `main` before each tag still saves them on `main`. A Windows or macOS cache that GitHub dropped after 7 days unused costs one cold OCCT build within the 150 minutes of `occt-platforms`, in CI or in the platforms run. Each such miss is recorded in open issue 16. The `bundle` job's `Swatinem/rust-cache` uses `save-if: ${{ github.ref == 'refs/heads/main' }}`, so only that dry run saves its Rust cache; `rust-platforms` saves its own the same way, debug builds kept apart from the bundle's release builds, and with `cache-on-failure: true` also after failing tests, so each run while its findings are being fixed restores the dependency builds of the one before instead of building them cold. Its post step also saves after a canceled job. A newer push that cancels it after a key miss can leave a partial cache (2.14, The move, choice 1). `full-rebuild` saves its own with the same two inputs: release builds of the benchmark's dependencies on `macos-15` (2.14). The apt archives of the browser installs and of the Tauri Linux packages are saved on `main` alone too (below). GitHub keeps at most 10 GB of caches per repository and drops the least recently used beyond it. CI uses its caches, the Windows and macOS ones among them, on every push. The ones dropped first are those that only the weekly platforms run and the release use: `full-rebuild`'s Rust cache and the `bundle` job's Rust caches (open issue 16). The `bundle` job's OCCT keys are those of `occt-native` and `occt-platforms`, which CI restores in every run. The Tauri Linux packages, installed with `sudo apt-get update && sudo apt-get install -y libwebkit2gtk-4.1-dev libxdo-dev libssl-dev librsvg2-dev` in `rust`, `desktop` and the Linux `bundle` job, are Tauri's documented prerequisites without the tray library (the app has no tray icon). The `rust` job needs them because `partsmith-desktop` is a workspace member.

**The apt cache** (2.4, 2.5, 2.14, ADR 0038's amendment of 2026-11-06, choices 7 and 8, Accepted (owner's rule of 2026-11-05)). Five jobs install Ubuntu packages with apt each time they run. `e2e` and both rows of `e2e-engines` install their browser's system packages with `pnpm exec playwright install --with-deps <browser>`, which runs `apt-get update` and then `apt-get install -y --no-install-recommends` with the packages that its version of `playwright-core` lists for the browser on the runner's Ubuntu (`installDependenciesLinux`). `rust`, `desktop` and `release.yml`'s Linux `bundle` row install the Tauri Linux packages with `sudo apt-get update && sudo apt-get install -y libwebkit2gtk-4.1-dev libxdo-dev libssl-dev librsvg2-dev`. apt-get downloads each package the image lacks from the first mirror of the runner's apt list, `azure.archive.ubuntu.com`, and keeps its `.deb` file in `/var/cache/apt/archives`. The cache relies on `apt-get`, which keeps downloaded archives. The `apt` command removes them by default (`Binary::apt::APT::Keep-Downloaded-Packages` is `0` in apt 2.8). The packages take about 21 MB for Chromium (21.1 MB in CI run 28), 50 MB for Firefox and 120 MB for WebKit (48.8 and 117 MB in platforms run 3), and 61 MB for the Tauri packages (173 packages and 61.2 MB in the CI run on wave H2's branch, 42218307369). 33.7 MB of the Tauri download are two packages of WebKitGTK from `noble-updates`: `libwebkit2gtk-4.1-0` (25.5 MB) and `libjavascriptcoregtk-4.1-0` (8.2 MB). Ubuntu updates them with each WebKitGTK security release, often every few weeks. Each set of packages has a cache of its own, named by `<name>`: `chromium`, `firefox`, `webkit` or `tauri`. Five steps run around the install, in this order. In `e2e-engines` they are exactly these, right after `pnpm install --frozen-lockfile` and right before the step that runs its tests. `e2e` has them at the same place, with `${{ matrix.browser }}` replaced by `chromium` (test 130).

```yaml
- name: Restore the apt archives of ${{ matrix.browser }}
  id: apt-archives
  uses: actions/cache/restore@v6.1.0
  with:
    path: ~/apt-archives
    key: apt-archives-ubuntu-24.04-${{ matrix.browser }}-${{ github.run_id }}
    restore-keys: apt-archives-ubuntu-24.04-${{ matrix.browser }}-
- name: Copy the apt archives into apt's cache
  if: steps.apt-archives.outputs.cache-matched-key != ''
  run: sudo cp ~/apt-archives/*.deb /var/cache/apt/archives/
- name: Install ${{ matrix.browser }} and its system packages
  timeout-minutes: 10
  run: pnpm exec playwright install --with-deps ${{ matrix.browser }}
- name: Copy the apt archives out of apt's cache
  id: copy-out
  if: github.ref == 'refs/heads/main'
  run: |
    set -o pipefail
    export LC_ALL=C
    dpkg-query -W -f '${Package}_${Version}_${Architecture}.deb\n' | sed 's/:/%3a/g' | sort > "$RUNNER_TEMP/apt-installed"
    find /var/cache/apt/archives -maxdepth 1 -name '*.deb' -printf '%f\n' | sort | comm -12 - "$RUNNER_TEMP/apt-installed" > "$RUNNER_TEMP/apt-current"
    mkdir -p ~/apt-archives
    find ~/apt-archives -maxdepth 1 -name '*.deb' -printf '%f\n' | sort | comm -23 "$RUNNER_TEMP/apt-current" - > "$RUNNER_TEMP/apt-downloaded"
    if [ -s "$RUNNER_TEMP/apt-downloaded" ]; then
      rm -rf ~/apt-archives
      mkdir ~/apt-archives
      (cd /var/cache/apt/archives && xargs -a "$RUNNER_TEMP/apt-current" cp -t ~/apt-archives)
      echo "downloaded=true" >> "$GITHUB_OUTPUT"
    fi
- name: Save the apt archives of ${{ matrix.browser }}
  if: steps.copy-out.outputs.downloaded == 'true'
  uses: actions/cache/save@v6.1.0
  with:
    path: ~/apt-archives
    key: ${{ steps.apt-archives.outputs.cache-primary-key }}
```

`rust` and `desktop` have the same five steps around their step `Install the Tauri Linux packages`, which keeps its name and its command and sets no `timeout-minutes` (Timeouts, below). In the two keys `${{ matrix.browser }}` is `tauri`. In the names of the restore and the save it is `the Tauri Linux packages` (test 132). In `rust` the five steps come right after `Free disk space` and right before the toolchain step. In `desktop` they come right after the restore of `.occt/native` and right before the toolchain step. `release.yml`'s `bundle` has the first three right after its checkout, each with `runner.os == 'Linux'` in its `if`, and no copy out and no save (The release bundle, below). Its step `Install the Tauri Linux packages and Ninja` becomes `Install the Tauri Linux packages`, with the command of `rust` and `desktop`. `ninja-build` moves to a step of its own, `Install Ninja`, right after the OCCT cache step (`id: occt`), with `if: runner.os == 'Linux' && steps.occt.outputs.cache-hit != 'true'` and `run: sudo apt-get install -y ninja-build`. It runs after the Tauri step's `apt-get update` (test 133). A comment above each install step says that its time is apt's download when the cache misses (open issue 16).

**The restore.** `actions/cache/restore` looks for the exact `key` first. A run's key exists only when a job of the same run saved it first: an earlier attempt, or for the Tauri set the other job (One entry for `rust` and `desktop`, below). Otherwise the action takes the newest entry whose key starts with the prefix in `restore-keys`, from the run's own ref or from `main` (a pull request also reads its base branch). `cache-hit` is `true` only for the exact key. `cache-matched-key` names the entry restored and is empty on a miss: `actions/cache/restore` v6.1.0 sets it only when it restores. On a hit, by the exact key or by the prefix, the second step copies the entry's files into apt's cache with `sudo`. The copies belong to `root` with mode 0644, like the files apt downloads itself. apt then downloads only the packages the entry lacks or holds at another version. Every saved entry holds at least one `.deb` file (below). The `cp` of a hit always has a file to copy.

**The copy out.** On `main`, after an install that passed, the fourth step works out what the install downloaded and builds the next entry. It runs no `sudo` and reads no secret. Its first line sets `pipefail`. GitHub runs a step with no `shell` as `bash -e {0}`, which fails the step only for the last command of a pipeline. Without `pipefail`, a failing `dpkg-query` or `find` would leave a list empty and the step would pass with no output (Errors, below). `dpkg-query` names the `.deb` file of each package version that dpkg has installed, as apt names it in its cache: `<package>_<version>_<architecture>.deb`, with the colon of an epoch written `%3a` (`libxdo3_1%3a3.20160805.1-5build1_amd64.deb` for `libxdo3` 1:3.20160805.1-5build1, one of the Tauri packages, open issue 16). The files of apt's cache with those names are the current set: the packages the install needed, at the versions it installed. A file of the current set that the restored folder lacks is one the install downloaded. When there is at least one, the step replaces `~/apt-archives` with the current set and writes `downloaded=true` to `$GITHUB_OUTPUT`. The fifth step then saves the folder under the run's key. When the install downloaded nothing (every package on the image or in the entry), the step writes no output and the save is skipped. The new entry leaves out the restored files whose versions are not installed: a version Ubuntu replaced, or a package the image now has at another version. It keeps a restored file whose package the image already holds at that version: dpkg lists that version as installed. apt skips such a file. It stays in each later entry until the image moves to another version of its package, and it costs only room in the cache. Leaving it out would need dpkg's list from before the install, made in a sixth step or in a longer copy into apt's cache, in every run (choice 8 (i)). An entry holds one version of each package. Its size is about that of the archives its install took (the `<total>` of the install's `Need to get <part>/<total>` line), plus the files it keeps of packages the image holds. The save runs right after the install, not in a post step. A test that fails later still leaves the entry. The post step of `actions/cache` saves only after a job that passed. The two steps run only when every step before them passed. A failed or timed-out install saves nothing. A pull request runs neither.

**Why the key rolls.** GitHub keeps an entry's content fixed under its key. An entry goes stale once Ubuntu updates one of its packages. Each hit then downloads the update from the mirror. In the CI run on wave H2's branch the slowest downloads were the ones that go stale first: `libwebkit2gtk-4.1-0` took 391 s and, after `Ign:70`, 294 s more, and `libjavascriptcoregtk-4.1-0` took 216 s (open issue 16). With a key that never changes, as wave H2's browser keys between two Playwright updates, an entry stays stale until a person deletes it. With the runner image's version in the key, every image roll (about weekly) is a miss that downloads the whole set from the mirror, and an entry stays stale until the next roll. With a month in the key, a miss comes every month and an entry can stay stale for a month. With the run id in the key and a save only after a download, the first run on `main` that downloads an update saves an entry that holds it. Every later run restores that entry. An entry is stale for at most one run on `main`. A pull request run before that downloads the same updates, as it would under any key. The key leaves out the image's version, the Playwright version, the package list and any period. apt takes a cached file only when the run's package lists name its version (What apt checks, below), and the next save keeps only the versions installed. An image roll, a Playwright update or a package added to a list costs only the packages that changed, once.

**Entries and the 10 GB.** Each save adds an entry next to the one it replaces. A restore takes the newest, and no run reads the older one again. GitHub drops an entry unused for 7 days. A run on `main` saves at most one entry for each set, and only after its install downloaded a package the entry lacked: an Ubuntu update of one of its packages, a runner image that no longer holds a package of the set, or a change of the package list. One entry of each of the four sets takes about 252 MB together (21, 50, 120 and 61 MB). If every set changed every day, the 7 days would hold 8 entries of each, about 2 GB of the 10 GB. How often Ubuntu updates the packages of each set is not measured. Open issue 16 records each save of the first weeks (test 131). GitHub keeps at most 10 GB of caches per repository and drops the least recently used beyond it. An entry that a newer one replaced is among them. Every CI run restores the newest entry of each set. Wave H2's browser entries, `apt-archives-ubuntu-24.04-<browser>-playwright-1.64.0`, start with the new prefixes. A restore takes them until a run on `main` saves a newer entry for the browser. GitHub drops them 7 days after their last restore.

**One entry for `rust` and `desktop`.** Both install the same four packages on `ubuntu-24.04`. They share the prefix `apt-archives-ubuntu-24.04-tauri-` and, in one run, the key. On `main` both find the same downloads and both try to save. The first save stores the entry. The second logs `Failed to save: Unable to reserve cache with key apt-archives-ubuntu-24.04-tauri-<run id>, another job may be creating this cache.` at info level and passes: `actions/cache/save` v6.1.0 reports a key that another job is writing, or that exists, as a reserve error. A job that starts after the other's save restores that entry by its exact key, downloads nothing and saves nothing. An entry for each job would hold the same files twice, and each job would download an update before its own save.

**The release bundle.** A tag run restores the entries saved on `main`, as a dry run from `main` does. The Linux row installs exactly the four packages of `rust` and `desktop` and restores their newest entry. It saves none. An entry that a tag run saves serves no later tag (its ref is the tag's). A dry run from `main` would save what CI's runs on `main` already save on every push. With `ninja-build` in the Tauri command, the row's set would differ from CI's by one package. In a step of its own on an OCCT miss, as in `occt-native` and `occt-wasm`, it leaves the two sets equal.

**The installs that keep no cache.** The `ninja-build` installs of a cold OCCT build in `occt-native`, `occt-wasm` and the Linux `bundle` row keep no cache and no step timeout. Each is one package of about 0.1 MB. It is installed only when an OCCT cache misses, before a build of an hour or more. A cache would add five steps to save seconds on a path that runs rarely.

**Timeouts.** The browser installs keep their 10 minutes (2.14, test 79). The Tauri installs set no `timeout-minutes`: their jobs' timeouts bound them, 60 minutes in `rust` and `desktop` and 150 in `bundle`. On a hit they download only what Ubuntu updated since the newest save on `main`. In the usual case that is at most the 33.7 MB of a WebKitGTK update, about 19 minutes at the 29.8 kB/s of the branch run of wave H2. A 10-minute step timeout would fail such a run. It would also have failed that branch run's install of 26 min 26 s, which passed. A failed job waits for a person's rerun. A slow install that passes only makes its run longer. A Tauri install that takes more than 10 minutes is recorded in open issue 16 with its run, job, `Need to get` line and rate. One that runs into its job's timeout is the mirror's, not a defect of the app or a test. It is recorded there too. A `rust` or `desktop` job is then rerun with `gh run rerun <run-id> --failed` while the run is the newest of its concurrency group (2.14). Neither job uploads OCCT's compiled code. The 12 hours of 2.13 (Kept a day longer) do not apply to them. A `bundle` row is rerun as 3.6 says.

**Errors.** Neither cache step fails its job (`actions/cache` v6.1.0). A restore that meets an error of the cache service logs it with `Failed to restore:`, as a warning (an error annotation for a server error). It acts as a miss: the install downloads from the mirror. A save that meets one logs it with `Failed to save:` the same way. The job passes. A save whose path does not exist warns `Path Validation Error:` and saves nothing. The copy out fails its job when any of its commands fails, one inside a pipeline included (`set -o pipefail`).

**Only `main` saves.** Only runs on `main` save an entry, as only they save the Rust caches of `rust-platforms` and the `bundle` job. A pull request, from a fork too, restores the newest entry that `main` saved for its set and saves none. On a miss (a run before `main` has saved any entry for the set, or after GitHub dropped them all), it installs from the mirror as before. Entries saved under a pull request's ref would serve only that pull request and take room from the caches that every run uses.

**The key.** The key names the job's runner, `ubuntu-24.04`. A `.deb` file is built for one Ubuntu release and one architecture. `runner.os` names only `Linux`. `<name>` keeps the sets apart, and a job restores only its own. The run id makes the key of each save new. A rerun attempt of a run has the run's key and restores what an earlier attempt saved.

**What apt checks.** apt 2.8, the apt of `ubuntu-24.04`, takes a file of `/var/cache/apt/archives` as downloaded when its name, which holds the package, its version and its architecture, is the one the package lists give, and its size is theirs (`pkgAcqArchive::QueueNext`). It does not hash the file. It removes a file of another size and downloads the package. A partial entry costs only the files it lacks or holds cut short. A file of another version has another name. apt leaves it in place and downloads the version the lists name. A stale entry costs the download of each update until the next save on `main`. An install on a hit that fails because apt or dpkg rejects a file under `/var/cache/apt/archives` is recorded in open issue 16 with its log lines. Every entry of its set is then deleted: each key that `gh cache list --key apt-archives-ubuntu-24.04-<name>-` lists, with `gh cache delete <key>`. A save carries the files of the entry before it forward, and an older entry can hold the same file. The next run misses and downloads the whole set from the mirror. An install on a hit that ends at a timeout, its step's or its job's, is the mirror's. It is recorded and rerun (Timeouts, above), and no entry is deleted. apt does not hash those files. The entry is only as sound as what writes it: runs on `main`, from files that apt checked against the signed package lists when a run on `main` first downloaded them. Later saves carry them forward by name and size, including a file whose package the image already holds at that version. The OCCT installs that CI links and the Rust caches rest on the same scope.

**The runner image.** The runner image's build ends with `apt-get clean`, which leaves `/var/cache/apt/archives` empty. None of its apt settings removes downloaded files (the build scripts `configure-apt.sh`, `configure-dpkg.sh` and `cleanup.sh` of actions/runner-images at be74a77, 2026-11-06). Docker's Ubuntu images remove them after each update and install (`/etc/apt/apt.conf.d/docker-clean`), this repository's container among them. The runner is a virtual machine. The first runs check the image on the runner (test 131). CI run 30 showed that it keeps apt's downloads and that the runner's user can read them (open issue 16). The copy into apt's cache runs before the install's `apt-get update`. A hook that removed downloaded files after an update, as `docker-clean` does, would empty the cache before the install. The first hit checks that too (test 131).

`.github/workflows/AGENTS.md` changes in two sentences and in no other. The first is the line on the moved jobs in Interface, which describes `e2e-engines`' install step: "(`pnpm exec playwright install --with-deps`, 10 minutes, with apt's downloaded archives restored before it and, on `main` after a miss, saved after it, as `e2e` installs Chromium, delivery.md 3.2)" becomes "(`pnpm exec playwright install --with-deps`, 10 minutes, with apt's downloaded archives restored before it and, on `main` when it downloaded a package they lacked, saved after it, as in `e2e` and around the Tauri Linux packages of `rust` and `desktop`, delivery.md 3.2)". The second is the promotion rule's exception: "The one exception is a browser install of `e2e` or `e2e-engines` ending at its 10-minute timeout: that is the Ubuntu mirror, not the code or a test, so it is recorded in delivery.md open issue 16 and the failed jobs are rerun with `gh run rerun <run-id> --failed` within 12 hours of the end of the run's `sources` job, or the whole run with `gh run rerun <run-id>` later, while the run is the newest of its concurrency group in `ci.yml` (delivery.md 2.14); the rerun attempt's result is then the run's, as `gh run list` reports it." becomes "The one exception is a browser install of `e2e` or `e2e-engines` ending at its 10-minute timeout, or a Tauri install of `rust` or `desktop` running into its job's 60 minutes: that is the Ubuntu mirror, not the code or a test, so it is recorded in delivery.md open issue 16 and the failed jobs are rerun with `gh run rerun <run-id> --failed` (for `e2e` and `e2e-engines`, which upload OCCT's compiled code, within 12 hours of the end of the run's `sources` job, or the whole run with `gh run rerun <run-id>` later) while the run is the newest of its concurrency group in `ci.yml` (delivery.md 2.14 and 3.2); the rerun attempt's result is then the run's, as `gh run list` reports it." The 12 hours stay with the browser installs: `rust` and `desktop` upload no artifact (2.13, Kept a day longer, and Timeouts, above). The timeouts rule stays as it is. The Tauri installs have no step timeout of their own.

**Disk space.** Each crate builds its integration tests as one test binary (open issue 9, ADR 0035): `autotests = false`, one `[[test]]` named `integration`, and `tests/integration.rs`, which declares the other `.rs` files directly in `tests/` and the helper directories as modules. Two test targets stay separate: the release-only budgets of `partsmith-features` and of `partsmith-recompute`, each crate's `budgets` (`tests/budgets.rs`), never timed beside the crate's other tests (`partsmith-recompute`'s also one at a time). The reference stability corpus, a third target behind the `stability-study` feature until every scenario passed, is part of `partsmith-recompute`'s `integration`. One binary per test file made 102 integration test executables that link OCCT statically in a default build, 42 to 112 MB each without debug info and 7.0 GB together. With Cargo's default debug info they outgrew the free space of an `ubuntu-24.04` runner, and the linker died with a bus error while writing its output (the first CI run, 2026-10-13). Merged, those 102 became 11 integration test executables, 46 to 112 MB each and 0.88 GB together; with the unit tests, a default build links OCCT into 21 test executables (those of `partsmith-occt-sys` and the eight crates that depend on it), 1.5 GB together (measured on 2026-10-22). The workflow still sets `CARGO_PROFILE_DEV_DEBUG: "0"` for every job, which keeps the test executables small and quick to link (panic messages keep their file and line; a developer who needs a debugger builds locally with debug info), and the `rust` job still first removes the preinstalled SDKs it does not use (`sudo rm -rf /usr/share/dotnet /usr/local/lib/android /opt/ghc /opt/hostedtoolcache/CodeQL`) and prints `df -h /` before the build and after the tests; dropping either waits for a CI run that measures the space it saves. In the CI run on wave H2's branch (42218307369), `df -h /` showed `/dev/root` of 145G with 104G available after `Free disk space` and 99G after the tests. The apt cache adds about 61 MB to the `rust` job's disk: the entry's files in `~/apt-archives`, which stay there while cargo builds. Their copies in `/var/cache/apt/archives` are the files apt keeps with or without the cache. The margin needs no step that deletes them.

`deny.toml` keeps its license allow list: every license in the Tauri dependency tree is already allowed (ADR 0014). Two RustSec advisories apply to the Linux GTK3 stack Tauri uses, RUSTSEC-2024-0429 (`glib` 0.18, unsound `VariantStrIter`) and RUSTSEC-2024-0370 (`proc-macro-error`, unmaintained, a build-time dependency of the gtk-rs 0.18 macros); `deny.toml` lists both in `[advisories] ignore` with that reason and a note to remove them when Tauri moves past gtk-rs 0.18 (section 8).

### 3.3 Cloudflare Pages deploy

1. The deploy runs only when both secrets are present. On forks and in repositories without them (including Dependabot pull requests, which get no secrets) the job succeeds with the notice of 2.4 and deploys nothing.
2. A push to `main` deploys with `--branch=main`; the Pages project's production branch is `main`, so that deployment becomes production.
3. A pull request deploys with `--branch=<head branch>`; Pages makes it a preview with a stable alias `https://<sanitized branch>.<subdomain>`, where `<subdomain>` is the project's `*.pages.dev` host (2.12), and a unique deployment URL. The alias URL is the `preview` environment's URL on the pull request and is written to the job summary.
4. The artifact deployed is exactly the `web-dist` that `web` checked (`check:dist`) and that `e2e` tested against the same core build.
5. The project name comes from the repository variable `CLOUDFLARE_PAGES_PROJECT`, else `partsmith`. Creating the project (`wrangler pages project create <name> --production-branch main`) and the token are one-time human steps (section 8).
6. Only this job builds what Pages serves. Cloudflare's own build image cannot build the WebAssembly core (it needs Emscripten and OCCT built for WebAssembly, longer than a Pages build may run), and `ui/public/core` is not in git, so a Pages build of `ui/` alone serves an app whose core fails to load. A project connected to the repository in the Cloudflare dashboard therefore keeps its automatic builds paused (Settings, Build, Branch control: "Enable automatic production branch deployments" off and preview branches None), and this job's Wrangler deploys go to it as to a project created with Wrangler.
7. Before Wrangler runs, the job checks the source stamp (2.13): a build whose `SOURCE.txt` does not name the run's commit is not deployed, and the job fails.
8. Then it checks the OCCT source archive (2.13): a build without a whole `occt-{OCCT_VERSION}-source.tar.gz` of the pinned OCCT commit is not deployed, and the job fails. Previews and production get the same check.
9. Then it checks the dependency source archives (2.13): a build whose `dependencies/SHA256SUMS` is missing, does not list the first part of both web archives or every part in the folder, or names a SHA-256 that a part does not have, or whose joined parts do not make a whole archive, is not deployed, and the job fails. Previews and production get the same check.
10. Then it checks Partsmith's source archive (2.13): a build without a whole `partsmith-source.tar.gz` whose tar names the run's commit is not deployed, and the job fails. Previews and production get the same check.

### 3.4 Generated files

The `schema` job fails when regenerating changes or adds a file: after `cargo run -p partsmith-cli -- schema --write` and `pnpm -C ui generate:protocol`, `git status --porcelain -- docs/format ui/src/generated` must print nothing. On failure it prints `::error::Generated files are out of date. Run: cargo run -p partsmith-cli -- schema --write && pnpm -C ui generate:protocol` and the diff (`git diff -- docs/format ui/src/generated`). cli.md test 53 (`schema --check` in the repository root) checks the same within `cargo test`; the job also covers the TypeScript types and names the fix.

### 3.5 The 25 MiB per-file gate

Cloudflare Pages serves files of at most 25 MiB each (technical design section 4). `pnpm check:dist` in the `web` job fails the build when any file in `ui/dist` is larger, naming each one, so a deploy can never silently drop or break the core.

**Measured sizes (2026-10-12).**

- `ui/dist` without the core (the latest local build): `assets/index-*.js.map` 1,045,216 bytes, `assets/index-*.js` 219,690 bytes, `assets/index-*.css` 4,356 bytes, `index.html` 467 bytes, `_headers` 90 bytes (409 bytes since ADR 0049, 2.7).
- The WebAssembly core: `ui/public/core/partsmith_core.wasm`, linked with the engine by `scripts/build-wasm.sh` (`wasm-release` profile: `opt-level = "s"`, fat LTO), is 18,050,908 bytes (17.2 MiB), about 7.8 MiB under the 25 MiB limit.
- The OCCT source archive that every deployment holds since 2026-11-04 (2.13): `occt-7.9.3-source.tar.gz` is 17,457,358 bytes (16.6 MiB), 8,757,042 bytes under the limit. It is the second largest file of the build, after the core.
- The parts of the dependency source archives in `dependencies/` (2.13): at most 25,165,824 bytes (24 MiB) each, 1 MiB under the limit, by construction. On 2026-11-04 the web build has five, three of that size, one of 7,876,608 bytes and one of 5,104,928 bytes. Since 2026-11-05 it has seven: five of that size and one of 14,694,400 bytes, the parts of `npm-packages-web.tar`, and the one part of `rust-crates-web.tar.gz`, 5,104,928 bytes.
- Partsmith's own source archive that every deployment holds since wave G (2.13): `partsmith-source.tar.gz` of `0ae7337` is 12,122,642 bytes (11.6 MiB), 14,091,758 bytes under the limit. It grows with the repository.
- The kernel alone: the largest kernel test module (the `partsmith-occt-sys` unit tests, which reach the whole facade), built with the same profile, is 14.08 MiB, 13.5 MiB after `wasm-opt -Oz`, 4.9 MiB gzip and 3.6 MiB brotli (ADR 0017).

At these sizes the core fits in one module: splitting it into lazily loaded modules is not needed now, and R2 is a fallback only (ADR 0017).

**The plan when the gate fails**, in order; each step is a decision recorded in an ADR with human review, not something CI does on its own:

1. Size options within one module: `-Oz` for Rust and for the OCCT build, Binaryen `-Oz` with `--converge`, and linking only the OCCT toolkits the engine calls. This changes the build flags of ADR 0003 and ADR 0009.
2. Lazily loaded modules (technical design section 4): a base kernel module, booleans and fillets, STEP I/O and mesh tools, each linked with only the OCCT toolkits it needs, loaded by the worker on first use. This changes the bridge of protocol.md 2.10 and ADR 0009.
3. Cloudflare R2 for any file still over the limit: a bucket on a custom domain serving content-hashed core files with `Cross-Origin-Resource-Policy: cross-origin` and CORS for the Pages origin (both needed under `require-corp`), uploaded by CI and loaded by the worker from a build-time base URL. It needs a domain, an R2 API token as a new secret, cost approval, and a ui.md change to the worker's `load` (ui.md 2.3).

For the OCCT source archive the core's steps do not apply. A pin whose archive is over the limit needs the ADR of 2.13 (Size and upload): the archive in parts that the web statement names, or another place the owner controls. Nor do they apply to Partsmith's source archive. A repository whose archive is over the limit needs an ADR too, such as parts like those of the dependency archives (2.13, Partsmith's source archive). The oversized-file message of section 4 stays the same for every file.

### 3.6 Desktop release

Matrix of the `bundle` job (desktop.md 3.8, test 81):

| `runner.os` | Runner | `--bundles` | Uploaded files |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Linux | `ubuntu-24.04` | `deb,rpm` | `target/release/bundle/deb/*.deb`, `rpm/*.rpm` |
| Windows | `windows-2025` | `nsis` | `target/release/bundle/nsis/*-setup.exe` |
| macOS | `macos-15` | `app,dmg` | `target/release/bundle/dmg/*.dmg` |

Steps per runner, all with `shell: bash` (Git Bash on Windows), in a job with `timeout-minutes: 150` (2.5):

1. Checkout. Linux: the Tauri packages, after the restore of the apt archives that `ci.yml` saved on `main` (3.2). Windows: `ilammy/msvc-dev-cmd@v1.13.0` (MSVC, CMake and Ninja from Visual Studio on `PATH`), then a step that writes `CARGO_TARGET_X86_64_PC_WINDOWS_MSVC_LINKER` (the `link.exe` next to `cl.exe`, the one CMake uses for OCCT) to `$GITHUB_ENV`, because Git Bash puts GNU `link` first on `PATH` and rustc takes the first `link.exe` when `VCINSTALLDIR` is set. macOS: `brew install ninja`.
2. OCCT native: `actions/cache@v6.1.0` with the key of 3.2; on a miss, on Linux `ninja-build` in the step `Install Ninja` (3.2), then `scripts/build-occt.sh native "$OCCT_ROOT"` with `OCCT_JOBS: 4` in the environment (so the script does not need `nproc`, which macOS lacks). On macOS the job sets `MACOSX_DEPLOYMENT_TARGET: "13.0"` for OCCT, Rust and the bundle (desktop.md 2.8).
3. Rust toolchain and `Swatinem/rust-cache@v2.9.2` with `save-if: ${{ github.ref == 'refs/heads/main' }}`, so only a dry run from `main` saves the Rust cache, where every tag run can restore it (3.2); pnpm and Node 22; `pnpm -C ui install --frozen-lockfile`; `pnpm -C ui build`.
4. `tauri-cli` from its cache, else `cargo install tauri-cli --version 2.12.2 --locked`.
5. `cargo tauri build --bundles <bundles> --ci` in `desktop/src-tauri`. On Windows, the step `Check the imports of the app` then checks `target/release/partsmith-desktop.exe`, the app the installer holds (The C runtime on Windows, below).
6. Copy the files of the table into one flat `bundles/` folder (each pattern expands unquoted, so a pattern that matches nothing fails the copy), and upload `bundles/` as `bundle-<runner.os>` (`retention-days: 7`, so a run's bundles do not stay downloadable for the repository's default of 90 days; `if-no-files-found: error`). Flat assets let `draft` run `gh release create` on `release/*` and write `SHA256SUMS` over plain file names.

`draft` then creates the draft release with every bundle, `THIRD-PARTY-NOTICES.txt` (2.9), Partsmith's source archive `partsmith-source.tar.gz` and the OCCT source archive `occt-{OCCT_VERSION}-source.tar.gz` (2.13), the parts of `rust-crates-desktop.tar.gz` and `npm-packages-desktop.tar` (2.13) and `SHA256SUMS`. It takes the source archives from the artifact `sources-desktop`, which the run's `sources` job uploaded before the bundles, and which the dry run's `sources` job already made once from the same lock files (2.5, 2.13). When any step of `draft` fails, `gh run rerun <id> --failed` runs `draft` again while the `bundle-*` artifacts of step 6 (7 days) and `sources-desktop` (8 days) last. After 7 days, `gh run rerun <id>` builds the bundles again. A failed `bundle` row is rerun with `gh run rerun <id> --failed` only within 12 hours of the end of the run's `sources` job. Later, `gh run rerun <id>` runs the whole run again, which uploads `sources-desktop` again too (2.13, Kept a day longer). A human downloads and tries the bundles, edits the notes, and publishes the release (technical design section 17: "a human approves each tagged version before it is published"). Nothing is signed: Windows SmartScreen warns and macOS needs the user's approval in System Settings, which the release notes state with the paragraph below.

**The unsigned-bundle paragraph**, word for word, at the top of every release's notes, above the notes that `--generate-notes` writes, in README.md (2.12) and in `.github/workflows/AGENTS.md` (test 82):

```text
These builds are not signed yet. On Windows, SmartScreen may say "Windows protected your PC": choose More info, then Run anyway. On macOS, the first open is blocked: open System Settings, then Privacy & Security, and choose Open Anyway for Partsmith. On Linux, the .deb and .rpm need glibc 2.39 or later and WebKitGTK 4.1, as on Ubuntu 24.04. To check a download, run sha256sum -c SHA256SUMS --ignore-missing in the folder that holds it.
```

**Release checklist**, kept in `.github/workflows/AGENTS.md` with this text (tests 82 and 83):

1. The source route of ADR 0034 is in place for this commit: the repository is public, so the tag holds the Partsmith source, and `draft` attaches Partsmith's source archive, the OCCT source archive and the dependency source archives (2.13); a release without them stays a draft.
2. CI passed on the `main` commit to release, including `check:notices` (2.4), and a run of the platforms workflow on `main` passed on that commit (2.14): the weekly one, or one started with `gh workflow run platforms.yml --ref main`; `gh run list --workflow platforms.yml --branch main --commit <sha> --status success` shows it; that run's summary shows the full-rebuild median within its budget, or an ADR the owner decided covers that median (2.14, ADR 0054).
3. The version is the same in the workspace `Cargo.toml` (`[workspace.package] version`) and `ui/package.json`, as `verify` checks; a version bump is its own pull request.
4. A release dry run (`workflow_dispatch` of `release.yml`) from `main` passed on that commit (test 13), and each bundle installed and started on its OS (the manual checks of desktop.md section 6).
5. Push the tag `vX.Y.Z` on that commit.
6. When `draft` finishes, download the assets, check them with `sha256sum -c SHA256SUMS`, and install each bundle once more.
7. Edit the draft's notes: the unsigned-bundle paragraph first, then the generated notes.
8. Publish the release.

**Prerequisites in the build scripts**, made by the implementer of `release.yml` and proven by CI's `occt-platforms` and `rust-platforms` jobs (2.14) and, for the bundles, a `workflow_dispatch` dry run on all three runners before the first tag: `scripts/build-occt.sh` passes `-DINSTALL_DIR_LAYOUT=Unix` on Windows, so the install has the `include/opencascade` and `lib` folders `partsmith-occt-sys/build.rs` expects, and the two settings of the static C runtime (below); the Windows build uses Ninja with MSVC from the developer environment; and the native OCCT and the shim must build with MSVC and with Apple Clang for arm64, which every run of those jobs has done since the first, on 2026-10-26 (open issue 1).

**The C runtime on Windows** (ADR 0038 option (i), the owner's decision of 2026-11-04, and ADR 0038's amendment of 2026-11-05, the owner's decision of that day; desktop.md 2.2). Every Windows build links the Visual C++ runtime (`libcmt`, `libvcruntime`) and the C++ standard library (`libcpmt`) statically. Every Windows program of the workspace takes the Universal C Runtime from Windows: the app, the desktop tests, the CLI that the examples runner runs, every other test executable and doc test, and the build scripts and proc macros. This is the hybrid C runtime that Microsoft documents for the Windows App SDK and that tauri-build's `staticVCRuntime` makes. No program imports a DLL of the Visual C++ Redistributable (`MSVCP140.dll`, `VCRUNTIME140.dll`, `CONCRT140.dll`, `VCOMP140.dll` or any other whose name step 3 of the check of the imports below matches). Each imports the Universal C Runtime of Windows (`api-ms-win-crt-*.dll`, the DLLs that step 4 looks for), part of Windows 10 and later, the Windows that WebView2 114 needs (desktop.md 1). The app runs on a Windows that does not have the redistributable. Four places make it, and they agree:

1. `scripts/build-occt.sh` adds `-DCMAKE_MSVC_RUNTIME_LIBRARY=MultiThreaded` and `-DCMAKE_POLICY_DEFAULT_CMP0091=NEW` to its CMake arguments after `-DINSTALL_DIR_LAYOUT=Unix`, in its branch for a `uname -s` that starts with `MINGW`, `MSYS` or `CYGWIN` (Git Bash on Windows). CMake reads `CMAKE_MSVC_RUNTIME_LIBRARY` only under policy CMP0091 of CMake 3.15, and OCCT 7.9.3 asks for CMake 3.10, which leaves the policy unset; the command line sets it. OCCT's own CMake files choose no runtime. The arguments on Linux and macOS and for WebAssembly stay as they were (test 99). The Universal C Runtime needs no change here. A `/MT` object names its runtime only through the default libraries it lists (`LIBCMT` and `OLDNAMES`, and `libcpmt` for the C++ standard library), and the final link decides which Universal C Runtime `libcmt.lib` reaches (item 2).
